<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:unrestricted-density"></a>
A convex body in $\mathbb R^n$ is a compact convex set with nonempty interior. A set $X\subset\mathbb R^n$ is *locally finite* if every bounded set contains only finitely many of its points. It gives a *translative covering* of $K$ when $K+X=\mathbb R^n$. Write $Q_R=[-R,R]^n$. We define the translative covering density by $$\label{eq:unrestricted-density}
 \theta_T(K)=\inf_{\substack{X\text{ locally finite}\\K+X=\mathbb R^n}}
 \mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(K)\limsup_{R\to\infty}
       \frac{\#(X\cap Q_R)}{(2R)^n}.$$ Thus the density measures the asymptotic volume of translates per unit volume, counting overlaps. It is not the fraction of space covered, which is one for every covering. No asymptotic density limit is assumed for an individual arrangement.

A *periodic translative covering* is a covering $$\mathbb R^n=K+\Lambda+T,$$ where $\Lambda$ is a full-rank lattice and $T$ is a finite set of distinct representatives modulo $\Lambda$. Its center intensity is $|T|/\det\Lambda$, and its density is $|T|\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(K)/\det\Lambda$. Put $$\theta_{\mathrm{per}}(K)=\inf\left\{
 \frac{|T|\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(K)}{\det\Lambda}:K+\Lambda+T=\mathbb R^n
 \right\}.$$ The infimum ranges over every lattice and every finite set $T$. In particular, there is no bound on the number of cosets. Allowing repeated representatives of the same coset gives the same infimum: deleting redundant representatives preserves the covering and does not increase its listed density. When we later retain indexed center lists, we count such repetitions explicitly. Both infima are finite: an interior cube of $K$ gives a covering by translates on a sufficiently fine cubic lattice.

The classical periodization principle gives <a id="eq:density-equivalence"></a>$$\label{eq:density-equivalence}
 \theta_T(K)=\theta_{\mathrm{per}}(K).$$ We prove the precise statement in Appendix <a href="#sec:periodization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:periodization">10</a>, including agreement with lower or upper centered-ball density and average covering multiplicity. See also Prosanov (Prosanov 2021, sec. 1.1 and Theorem 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Prosanov2021PackingCovering](#ref-Prosanov2021PackingCovering)], who attributes the principle to Rogers. These are equalities of optimal densities; the lower and upper densities of an individual nonperiodic arrangement need not agree.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There are absolute constants $c>0$ and $n_0$ such that, for every integer $n\ge n_0$, some centrally symmetric convex body $K_n\subset\mathbb R^n$ satisfies $$\theta_T(K_n)>c n\log n.$$*

</div>

<a id="eq:rogers"></a>
Thus there is no absolute constant $C$ such that $\theta_T(K)\le Cn$ for every convex body in every dimension, even if the bodies are required to be centrally symmetric. This answers negatively the universal linear-bound question recorded by Naszódi (Naszódi 2018, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Naszodi2018Flavors](#ref-Naszodi2018Flavors)]. Rogers (Rogers 1957) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Rogers1957Coverings](#ref-Rogers1957Coverings)] proved that every convex body in dimension $n\ge3$ has a periodic translative covering of density at most $$\label{eq:rogers}
 n\log n+n\log\log n+5n.$$ Together with Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, this shows that the supremum of covering density over convex bodies has order $n\log n$. The same order holds when one restricts the bodies to be centrally symmetric. The constant in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is not optimized. The bodies may moreover be chosen to be centrally symmetric polytopes with rational vertices, with the same constant $c$; see Corollary <a href="#cor:rational-polytope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:rational-polytope">15</a>.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Antecedents and the additional uniformity

Rogers’ theorem supplies a universal upper bound for arbitrary convex bodies. Fejes Tóth (Fejes Tóth 2009) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[FejesToth2009Covering](#ref-FejesToth2009Covering)] sharpened its lower-order term to obtain density $n(\log n+\log\log n+1+o(1))$ using only $O(\log n)$ cosets of a common lattice. A finite union of lattice cosets has substantially more freedom than one lattice, even with this bound on the number of cosets. On the lower-bound side, the classical work of Coxeter, Few and Rogers (Coxeter, Few, and Rogers 1959) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[CoxeterFewRogers1959Spheres](#ref-CoxeterFewRogers1959Spheres)] gives an order-$n$ obstruction already for unrestricted coverings by equal Euclidean balls. For Euclidean balls, Dumer (Dumer 2007, Corollary 2) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Dumer2007Spheres](#ref-Dumer2007Spheres)] gives the upper bound $(1/2+o(1))n\log n$. The gap for Euclidean balls remains; our theorem determines the optimal order when the convex body may also be chosen.

The proof builds on the random-slab construction and Poisson witness method of Li and Liu (Li and Liu 2026, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[LiLiu2026Lattice](#ref-LiLiu2026Lattice)]. They prove an order-$n\log n$ lower bound for *lattice* covering density, in which the centers form a single lattice. Their common-intersection estimates, terminal clustering of caps, and overlap-diagram bounds provide the local probabilistic machinery used here. We give the necessary versions and their proofs, including the continuous form of Janson’s inequality.

The additional issue is to exclude every finite union of lattice cosets. A net of lattice bases cannot encode an arbitrary number of cosets. We instead localize an arrangement in a fixed Euclidean window, keeping every center with its multiplicity, and round the resulting list to a finite grid. Periodic averaging controls this list even when the lattice has very short periods or a highly skew fundamental domain. The resulting finite class is fixed before the body is sampled, so it includes arrangements chosen after the body is known. A sufficiently strong lower-tail bound then excludes all of them simultaneously.

For comparison, Bukh, Gao, Liu, Pikhurko, and Sun (Bukh et al. 2025, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BukhEtAl2025Random](#ref-BukhEtAl2025Random)] show that, in their packing-torus model, Poisson centers at a specified density of order $n\log n$ fail to cover a given body with probability tending to one. Their random-centers barrier has different quantifiers: an exceptional arrangement might still cover efficiently. Here the body is random and the conclusion excludes every periodic arrangement for one realization of that body.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof outline

Intersect a slightly enlarged Euclidean ball with symmetric slabs whose unit normals are sampled by a Poisson process. The normalization gives this body a substantial volume with positive probability. A hypothetical covering of low center intensity supplies, after localization and rounding, a finite center list with many suitable target points in a small window. We must show that each such list is very unlikely to cover that window.

At a target $y$, only centers $p$ within the outer ball’s radius of $y$ can cover it. For each such center, the residual vector $y-p$ determines the antipodal caps of slab normals that would remove $y$ from that translate. A hole appears if the Poisson process hits at least one cap in each pair. Nearly coincident cutting sets are strongly dependent. Following Li and Liu, we group these conditions into blocks and choose one common subset of normals for each block. Assigning distinct Poisson points to these subsets certifies a hole. The block construction controls the complete overlap sum of these certificates, including their self-overlaps.

The targets are chosen at two angular scales. For every pair of distinct targets, every pair of their relevant residual directions is separated at a scale of order $\sqrt{\log n/n}$, identifying opposite directions because the slabs are symmetric. The cap estimates then bound the dependence between their hole certificates. The body lies in a ball whose radius is only slightly larger than the slab half-width, so its cutting caps have a small fixed angular radius. At a larger fixed separation, all cutting caps belonging to one target are disjoint from those belonging to the other. A cylinder-volume estimate lets us arrange this stronger separation for all but an exponentially small fraction of target pairs, while retaining the finer separation for every pair.

Let $R$ denote the density lower bound sought. We can choose $\exp(c_1n\log n)$ targets for a fixed $c_1>0$, enough to absorb a self-overlap cost $\exp(CR)$ when $R=c n\log n$ and $c$ is sufficiently small. The two angular scales control the remaining overlap between distinct targets. Janson’s lower-tail inequality turns these bounds into a covering probability that is doubly exponentially small in $n$, with decay strong enough to dominate the number of rounded patterns. A second-moment bound would not give the required decay.

Section <a href="#sec:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:construction">2</a> defines the body and the constants. Section <a href="#sec:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:localization">3</a> constructs the finite pattern class. Sections <a href="#sec:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:caps">4</a>–<a href="#sec:poisson" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:poisson">6</a> establish the cap, block, and Poisson estimates. Section <a href="#sec:targets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:targets">7</a> proves the fixed-pattern bound, and Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">8</a> completes the proof. Section <a href="#sec:encoding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:encoding">9</a> gives rational-polytope and rational-normal versions of the construction. Appendix <a href="#sec:periodization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:periodization">10</a> proves the density equivalence that transfers the periodic exclusion to unrestricted coverings.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Notation

Logarithms are natural, and $[t]_+=\max\{t,0\}$. We write $|x|$ for Euclidean norm, $B_r=\{x\in\mathbb R^n:|x|\le r\}$, $B=B_1$, and $\omega_n=\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(B)$. For measurable sets in $\mathbb R^n$, $|A|$ also denotes Lebesgue volume; for finite lists and index sets it denotes cardinality. Surface measure $\sigma$ on $S^{n-1}$ is normalized to have total mass one. For nonzero vectors we use the projective angle $$\angle(x,z)=\arccos\frac{|x\cdot z|}{|x||z|}\in[0,\pi/2].$$ Lists are indexed: two entries can occupy the same point or define the same cap. Every sum and cardinality over such a list retains its multiplicities. Constants implicit in $O(\cdot)$ depend only on parameters explicitly fixed before $n$ tends to infinity.

<a id="sec:construction"></a>
### The random body

<a id="eq:fixed-constants"></a>
<a id="eq:rates"></a>
Fix the numerical constants $$\label{eq:fixed-constants}
 \varepsilon=2^{-30},\qquad a=1+\varepsilon,\qquad
 D=2^{-10}=\varepsilon^{1/3},\qquad v=\frac1{64}.$$ For each sufficiently large $n$, put <a id="eq:radii"></a>$$\label{eq:radii}
 \eta=n^{-4},\qquad b=a(1+1/n),\qquad
 r_-=a(1-10\log n/n).$$ The small window is $B_D$. Its size relative to $a$ will govern the number of patterns, through $$\label{eq:rates}
 \lambda_0=\log(1+D/a),\qquad
 \gamma=\log\frac{D}{8\sqrt\varepsilon}-2\lambda_0
        =\log4-2\lambda_0.$$ Since $\lambda_0<D=2^{-10}$, we have $\gamma>3\lambda_0$. These constants are fixed; none tends to zero with dimension.

<a id="eq:poisson-measure"></a>
<a id="eq:body"></a>
<a id="eq:buffered-body"></a>
Set $$\label{eq:poisson-measure}
 M=\bigl(\sigma\{u:|u\cdot e_1|>1/a\}\bigr)^{-1},\qquad \mu=M\sigma,$$ and let $\Pi$ be a Poisson process on $S^{n-1}$ with intensity $\mu$. Equivalently, sample a Poisson random integer of mean $M$, then that many independent $\sigma$-distributed unit normals. Define $$\begin{aligned}
 K&=bB\cap\bigcap_{u\in\Pi}\{x:|u\cdot x|\le1\},\label{eq:body}\\
 K^+&=(b+3\eta)B\cap\bigcap_{u\in\Pi}\{x:|u\cdot x|\le1+3\eta\}.
 \label{eq:buffered-body}
\end{aligned}$$ The enlarged body $K^+$ absorbs the displacement of centers during rounding. Since $M<\infty$, the list of normals is finite almost surely. Both bodies are compact and centrally symmetric, and $B\subset K$, so $K$ has nonempty interior.

<a id="stmt-lem-volume"></a>
<div id="lem:volume" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2**. *The body in <a href="#eq:body" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:body">[eq:body]</a> satisfies $$\mathbb P\{|K|>2v\omega_n a^n\}
 \ge \frac{\exp(-1)-2v}{3-2v}>0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-volume"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-volume</summary>

*Proof.* If $|x|\le a$, the $\mu$-measure of normals with $|u\cdot x|>1$ is at most one by rotational invariance and <a href="#eq:poisson-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:poisson-measure">[eq:poisson-measure]</a>. The probability that there is no such Poisson normal is therefore at least $\exp(-1)$. Integrating over $aB$ gives $\mathbb E|K|\ge\exp(-1)\omega_n a^n$. On the other hand, $|K|\le\omega_n b^n<3\omega_n a^n$. Thus $X=|K|/(\omega_n a^n)$ has $0\le X\le3$ and $\mathbb E X\ge\exp(-1)$. Writing $p=\mathbb P(X>2v)$, we have $\mathbb E X\le2v(1-p)+3p$, which proves the claim. ◻

</details>

We shall rule out coverings with center intensity at most <a id="eq:intensity-threshold"></a>$$\label{eq:intensity-threshold}
 \rho_0=\frac{R}{2v\omega_n a^n},$$ where $R$ is a positive parameter. This threshold does not depend on the realized volume. Once every such covering is excluded, the definition of $\theta_{\mathrm{per}}$ gives $\theta_{\mathrm{per}}(K)\ge\rho_0|K|$. The strict volume event in Lemma <a href="#lem:volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:volume">2</a> will then imply $\theta_{\mathrm{per}}(K)>R$, including strictness at the infimum.

<a id="sec:localization"></a>
### Reducing all periodic coverings to finitely many patterns

We first remove the lattice and its fundamental domain from the subsequent probability estimate. A periodic arrangement of bounded center intensity has a translated ball containing relatively few centers and many points with controlled residual vectors. Rounding those centers produces a list in a fixed finite family. The argument counts every occurrence of a center, so it applies to arbitrarily short or skew periods and to coinciding cosets.

<a id="eq:local-grid"></a>
<a id="eq:rounded-inner"></a>
<a id="eq:rounded-weight"></a>
Use the fixed parameters $a,D,v,b,r_-,\eta$ from the preceding section, and let $1\le R\le n^2$. Define $$C_{\mathrm{loc}}=\frac{1000}{v},\qquad
 w_a(r)=1+n[1-r^2/a^2]_+ \quad (r\ge0),
 \qquad h_n=\frac{\eta}{100n}.$$ The weight $w_a$ records the radial cost of a center near a target point. Our finite alphabet and maximum list length are $$\label{eq:local-grid}
 \mathcal Q_n=h_n\mathbb Z^n\cap B_{D+b+5\eta},\qquad
 N_n=\left\lceil\frac{20R}{v}
       \left(\frac{D+b+5\eta}{a}\right)^n\right\rceil.$$ An ordered list $P=(p_1,\ldots,p_m)$ over $\mathcal Q_n$ may contain repeated entries. Define $G_P\subseteq B_D$ to consist of the points $y$ satisfying the following three conditions: <a id="eq:rounded-count"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 |y-p_j|&\ge r_--2\eta &&(1\le j\le m),\label{eq:rounded-inner}\\
 \#\{j:|y-p_j|\le b+3\eta\}&\le2C_{\mathrm{loc}}R,
       \label{eq:rounded-count}\\
 \sum_{j:\,|y-p_j|\le b+3\eta}w_a(|y-p_j|)
       &\le2C_{\mathrm{loc}}R.\label{eq:rounded-weight}
\end{aligned}$$ The count condition follows from the weight condition because $w_a\ge1$; we display both to identify the cardinality and cost bounds used later. All sums count indices, including repeated entries. Set <a id="eq:pattern-family"></a>$$\label{eq:pattern-family}
 \mathcal F_n=
 \left\{P\in\bigcup_{m=0}^{N_n}\mathcal Q_n^m:
              |G_P|\ge\frac14|B_D|\right\}.$$ The dependence of $N_n$ and $\mathcal F_n$ on $R$ is suppressed in the notation. Empty lists are allowed, with all their sums equal to zero.

<a id="stmt-prop-localization"></a>
<div id="prop:localization" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:pattern-entropy"></a>
**Proposition 3** (Deterministic localization). *For all sufficiently large $n$, uniformly for $1\le R\le n^2$, the family $\mathcal F_n$ has the following properties. With $\lambda_0=\log(1+D/a)$, $$\label{eq:pattern-entropy}
 N_n\le n^{C_1}\exp(\lambda_0n),\qquad
 \log|\mathcal F_n|
       \le\exp\bigl(\lambda_0n+O(\log n)\bigr),$$ where $C_1$ and the implicit constant depend only on the fixed parameters.*

<a id="eq:localized-cover"></a>
*Let $\mathcal U\subset S^{n-1}$ be any finite set, and put $$K=bB\cap\bigcap_{u\in\mathcal U}\{x:|u\cdot x|\le1\},\qquad
 K^+=(b+3\eta)B\cap
          \bigcap_{u\in\mathcal U}\{x:|u\cdot x|\le1+3\eta\}.$$ Suppose that $\Lambda$ is any full-rank lattice, $T\subset\mathbb R^n$ is finite, and $$\frac{|T|}{\det\Lambda}\le
       \rho_0:=\frac{R}{2v\omega_na^n}.$$ If $K+\Lambda+T$ covers $\mathbb R^n$, there is a list $P=(p_1,\ldots,p_m)\in\mathcal F_n$ such that $$\label{eq:localized-cover}
 B_D\subseteq\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}(p_j+K^+).$$ The constructed list in fact satisfies $|G_P|\ge|B_D|/2$.*

<a id="eq:local-residual-budget"></a>
*For every $P\in\mathcal F_n$ and $y\in G_P$, list all residual vectors $x_i=y-p_j$ with $|y-p_j|\le b+3\eta$, retaining their indices, and denote their number by $m_y$. Then $$\label{eq:local-residual-budget}
 \begin{gathered}
 r_--2\eta\le |x_i|\le b+3\eta,\qquad
 m_y\le2C_{\mathrm{loc}}R,\qquad
 \sum_{i=1}^{m_y}w_a(|x_i|)\le2C_{\mathrm{loc}}R,\\
 m_y+\sum_{i=1}^{m_y}w_a(|x_i|)\le4C_{\mathrm{loc}}R.
 \end{gathered}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-localization"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-localization</summary>

*Proof.* We prove the localization and rounding assertions first, then count the resulting lists. All dimension thresholds in the proof are uniform for $1\le R\le n^2$.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### Averaging the full multiset of centers.

<a id="eq:periodic-averaging"></a>
Give each center an index $(t,\ell)\in T\times\Lambda$, at position $t+\ell$. Distinct indices remain distinct even if they have the same position. This multiset is locally finite because $T$ is finite and $\Lambda$ is discrete. Write $\rho=|T|/\det\Lambda$, and let $F$ be a fundamental parallelepiped of $\Lambda$. For every nonnegative integrable function $f$, $$\label{eq:periodic-averaging}
 \frac1{|F|}\int_F
       \sum_{(t,\ell)\in T\times\Lambda} f(y-t-\ell)\,dy
       =\rho\int_{\mathbb R^n}f(x)\,dx.$$ Indeed, Tonelli’s theorem permits the sums to be interchanged with the integral. For each $t$, the sets $F-t-\ell$, $\ell\in\Lambda$, partition $\mathbb R^n$ up to null sets. This proves the identity without any restriction on the shape or size of $F$.

<a id="eq:unrounded-weight"></a>
Put $\beta=b+4\eta$. Call a point $y\in\mathbb R^n$ unrounded-good if there is no center occurrence at distance less than $r_-$, at most $C_{\mathrm{loc}}R$ occurrences have distance at most $\beta$, and $$\label{eq:unrounded-weight}
 \sum_{(t,\ell):\,|y-t-\ell|\le\beta}
         w_a(|y-t-\ell|)\le C_{\mathrm{loc}}R.$$ Let $G$ be this periodic measurable set. These conditions respectively exclude short residuals, bound the number of relevant residuals, and bound their total radial cost. Using the full ball ensures that every index entering a rounded test is counted before rounding.

For a uniform point modulo $\Lambda$, the expected number of occurrences in its open $r_-$-ball is at most $$\rho\omega_n r_-^n
 \le\frac{R}{2v}
       \left(1-\frac{10\log n}{n}\right)^n
 \le\frac{R n^{-10}}{2v}
 \le\frac1{200}$$ for large $n$. Hence the first condition fails with probability at most $1/200$. Also $$\left(\frac\beta a\right)^n
 \le\exp(1+4/n^3)<3$$ for large $n$, so the expected outer count is at most $3R/(2v)$. For the weighted condition, polar integration gives the exact identity <a id="eq:weighted-ball-integral"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{B_\beta}w_a(|x|)\,dx
 &=\omega_n\beta^n
      +n\int_{B_a}(1-|x|^2/a^2)\,dx\notag\\
 &=\omega_n\beta^n+\frac{2n}{n+2}\omega_na^n
 <5\omega_na^n.\label{eq:weighted-ball-integral}
\end{aligned}$$ Equation <a href="#eq:periodic-averaging" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:periodic-averaging">[eq:periodic-averaging]</a> therefore bounds the expected weighted sum by $5R/(2v)$. Markov’s inequality bounds the two remaining failure probabilities by $3/2000$ and $5/2000$, respectively. Consequently <a id="eq:periodic-good-volume"></a>$$\label{eq:periodic-good-volume}
 \frac{|G\cap F|}{|F|}
 \ge1-\frac1{200}-\frac3{2000}-\frac5{2000}
 >\frac{99}{100}.$$

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
###### One window with both required bounds.

For $z$ uniform modulo $\Lambda$, let $M(z)$ count all center occurrences in $z+B_{D+b+4\eta}$. Equations <a href="#eq:periodic-averaging" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:periodic-averaging">[eq:periodic-averaging]</a> and <a href="#eq:local-grid" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-grid">[eq:local-grid]</a> imply $$\mathbb{E} M(z)
 \le\frac{R}{2v}\left(\frac{D+b+4\eta}{a}\right)^n
 \le\frac{N_n}{40}.$$ Moreover, periodicity and Fubini’s theorem give $$\mathbb{E}\frac{|(z+B_D)\setminus G|}{|B_D|}
 =1-\frac{|G\cap F|}{|F|}\le\frac1{100}.$$ Both expressions concern the entire Euclidean window, including all its overlapping period copies. Markov’s inequality gives $$\mathbb{P}\{M(z)>N_n\}\le\frac1{40},\qquad
 \mathbb{P}\{|(z+B_D)\setminus G|>|B_D|/2\}\le\frac1{50}.$$ The sum is less than one. Fix a slide $z$ outside these two exceptional events, and retain every occurrence in $z+B_{D+b+4\eta}$. There are at most $N_n$ such occurrences, and at least half of $z+B_D$ lies in $G$.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
###### Rounding and transferring coverage.

Translate this window and the retained centers by $-z$, and round every center coordinatewise to the nearest point of $h_n\mathbb Z^n$, using a fixed rule for ties. Each center moves a distance at most <a id="eq:rounding-error"></a>$$\label{eq:rounding-error}
 \delta_n=\frac{\sqrt n h_n}{2}
         =\frac{\eta}{200\sqrt n}\le\frac\eta{200}.$$ The rounded centers belong to $\mathcal Q_n$; enumerate them as an ordered list $P$, retaining all repeated entries.

We check that every $y\in B_D$ with $y+z\in G$ belongs to $G_P$. Every unrounded residual has length at least $r_-$, so every rounded one has length at least $r_--\delta_n\ge r_--2\eta$. A rounded residual of length at most $b+3\eta$ comes from an unrounded residual of length at most $$b+3\eta+\delta_n<b+4\eta.$$ Thus at most $C_{\mathrm{loc}}R$ rounded indices are relevant. This strict separation of the two outer radii ensures that every index entering a rounded test was counted before rounding.

The function $w_a$ is globally Lipschitz on $[0,\infty)$ with constant $2n/a$. Its derivative has absolute value $2nr/a^2\le2n/a$ for $0<r<a$, and it is constant for $r>a$. If $r$ and $r'$ are the unrounded and rounded lengths of a relevant residual, then $$w_a(r')\le w_a(r)+\frac{2n\delta_n}{a}
          \le\left(1+\frac{2n\delta_n}{a}\right)w_a(r),$$ where the last step uses $w_a(r)\ge1$. Summing over the relevant indices, all of which were included in <a href="#eq:unrounded-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unrounded-weight">[eq:unrounded-weight]</a>, gives $$\sum_{j:\,|y-p_j|\le b+3\eta}w_a(|y-p_j|)
 \le\left(1+\frac{2n\delta_n}{a}\right)C_{\mathrm{loc}}R
 \le2C_{\mathrm{loc}}R.$$ This verifies <a href="#eq:rounded-inner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounded-inner">[eq:rounded-inner]</a>–<a href="#eq:rounded-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounded-weight">[eq:rounded-weight]</a>. In particular, $|G_P|\ge|B_D|/2$ and $P\in\mathcal F_n$.

Now assume that the original periodic arrangement covers by $K$. For any point of $z+B_D$, every center whose translate of $K$ contains that point lies in $z+B_{D+b}$ because $K\subseteq bB$. Such a center was retained. Moving it by at most $\delta_n$ changes the residual norm by at most $\delta_n$ and each scalar product with a unit normal by at most $\delta_n$. Hence its rounded translate of $K^+$ contains the translated point of $B_D$. This proves <a href="#eq:localized-cover" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:localized-cover">[eq:localized-cover]</a> for every point of the closed window.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
###### Counting the lists.

Write $J_n=|\mathcal Q_n|$. A containing cube gives $$J_n\le\left(1+\frac{2(D+b+5\eta)}{h_n}\right)^n,
 \qquad \log J_n=O(n\log n).$$ Since $b=a(1+1/n)$, $\eta=n^{-4}$, and $1\le R\le n^2$, $$\log N_n=\lambda_0n+O(\log n).$$ This proves the first bound in <a href="#eq:pattern-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pattern-entropy">[eq:pattern-entropy]</a>. Counting ordered lists allows all multiplicities and gives $$|\mathcal F_n|\le\sum_{m=0}^{N_n}J_n^m
       \le(N_n+1)J_n^{N_n}.$$ The two preceding estimates give the second bound in <a href="#eq:pattern-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pattern-entropy">[eq:pattern-entropy]</a>. Finally, <a href="#eq:local-residual-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-residual-budget">[eq:local-residual-budget]</a> follows directly from the definition of $G_P$ and $w_a\ge1$. ◻

</details>

The family $\mathcal F_n$ depends only on the fixed numerical parameters, $n$, and $R$. Proposition <a href="#prop:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:localization">3</a> holds for every choice of the slab normals and every periodic covering of the required intensity. Thus a probability bound uniform over this finite family also controls centers chosen after the body is known. The next sections obtain such a bound by finding a point of $G_P$ missed by all translates in a fixed list.

<a id="sec:caps"></a>
### Intersections of spherical caps

We need two properties of the cutting caps: separated caps have small overlap, whereas a collection of nearby caps has a substantial common intersection. For a target $y\in G_P$ and a relevant center $p$, write $x=y-p$. The set $$C(x)=\{u\in S^{n-1}:|u\cdot x|>1+3\eta\}$$ consists of the normals whose slabs exclude $y$ from $p+K^+$. Figure <a href="#fig:residual-cap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:residual-cap">1</a> illustrates this correspondence. The estimates below allow the slightly more general thresholds needed to control these caps uniformly.

<figure id="fig:residual-cap">

<figcaption>Plane-section schematic of a residual and its cutting cap. The shaded antipodal caps lie beyond the two threshold hyperplanes $u ⋅ x =  ± (1+3η)$. A sampled normal in $C(x)$ excludes the target $y$ from $p + K^{+}$. The drawing does not depict high-dimensional cap measure. For a block of residuals, one normal in their common subset cuts them all; assigning distinct normals to all blocks is a sufficient certificate of a hole.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-residual-cap"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:residual-cap`)

```latex
  \fill[red!18] (35:1.4) arc (35:-35:1.4) -- cycle;
  \fill[red!18] (145:1.4) arc (145:215:1.4) -- cycle;
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 9 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Left: two points labeled $p$ and $y$ connected by a directed vector arrow labeled $x = y - p$ pointing from $p$ to $y$, with the caption text "center and target in physical space" below. Right: a circle representing the sphere labeled "normal sphere", with two vertical dashed lines cutting off shaded antipodal caps on the left and right. From the center of the sphere, a horizontal arrow points rightward along $x/|x|$ through the sphere boundary, and a second arrow points to a vector $u$ on the sphere inside the right shaded cap. Below the sphere is the text "cutting normals $C(x)$" and "$|u \cdot x| > 1 + 3\eta$".
```

The estimates adapt Li–Liu’s cap and common-lens arguments (Li and Liu 2026, Lemma 6.1 and Lemmas 9.2–9.3) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[LiLiu2026Lattice](#ref-LiLiu2026Lattice)]. In our thin radial shell, a bisector argument gives a short proof of the overlap estimate with a constant prefactor. Keeping that prefactor independent of dimension will be important when we sum overlaps.

<a id="stmt-lem-caps"></a>
<div id="lem:caps" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:caps-cost"></a>
<a id="eq:caps-affinity"></a>
<a id="eq:caps-lens"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Cap estimates). *Fix $1<a<2$ and $A,Q,B_0>0$. There exist constants $C_a,c_a'>0$, $C_{a,A}>0$, and $L=L(a)>0$ such that the following holds for all sufficiently large $n$, depending on $a,A,Q,B_0$. Let $\sigma$ be normalized surface measure on $S^{n-1}$, and set $$M=\sigma\{u:|u\cdot e_1|>1/a\}^{-1},\qquad \mu=M\sigma.$$ Let $x_1,\ldots,x_m\in\mathbb{R}^n$ and $\epsilon_1,\ldots,\epsilon_m\in\mathbb{R}$, where $1\le m\le n^2$, satisfy $$a\left(1-\frac{A\log n}{n}\right)-Qn^{-4}
 \le |x_i|\le a(1+1/n)+Qn^{-4},\qquad
 |\epsilon_i|\le Qn^{-4}.$$ Define $$C_i=\{u\in S^{n-1}:|u\cdot x_i|>1+\epsilon_i\},\quad
 c_i=\mu(C_i),\quad \bar c_i=\min(c_i,1/2),\quad
 s_i=-\log\bar c_i,$$ and, using projective angles, define $$d(i,j)=\sqrt n\,\angle(x_i,x_j)+\sqrt{|s_i-s_j|},\qquad
 r(S)=\min_{k\in S}\max_{i\in S}d(k,i)^2$$ for each nonempty set $S$ of indices. Then $$\begin{aligned}
 n^{-C_{a,A}}\le c_i&\le C_a,
 &s_i&\le C_a\bigl(1+n[1-|x_i|^2/a^2]_+\bigr),
 \label{eq:caps-cost}\\
 \frac{\mu(C_i\cap C_j)}{\sqrt{\bar c_i\bar c_j}}
 &\le C_a\exp\{-c_a'd(i,j)^2\}.
 \label{eq:caps-affinity}
\end{aligned}$$ The function $d$ is a pseudometric on the indexed list. If $r(S)\le B_0\log n$ and $i_0\in S$ minimizes the raw intensity $c_i$, then $$\mu\left(\bigcap_{i\in S}C_i\right)
 \ge e^{-L(1+r(S)+\log|S|)}\bar c_{i_0}.
 \label{eq:caps-lens}$$ In particular, $L$ is independent of $A,Q,B_0$; these parameters may affect the starting dimension.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-caps"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-caps</summary>

*Proof.* We first record one-dimensional estimates, then prove the overlap and common-intersection bounds. Constants with a subscript $a$ below may change from line to line, but depend only on $a$.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### One-cap probabilities and radial costs.

<a id="eq:caps-mills"></a>
<a id="eq:caps-hazard"></a>
Write $t_*=1/a$, $e_i=x_i/|x_i|$, and $\tau_i=(1+\epsilon_i)/|x_i|$. For a unit vector $e$, let $$F_n(t)=\sigma\{u:u\cdot e>t\}
 =k_n\int_t^1(1-v^2)^{(n-3)/2}\,dv,\qquad 0<t<1,$$ where $k_n$ is the normalization constant. Substitution of $z=1-v^2$, using $1\le1/v\le1/t$, gives $$\frac{k_n}{n-1}(1-t^2)^{(n-1)/2}
 \le F_n(t)\le
 \frac{k_n}{(n-1)t}(1-t^2)^{(n-1)/2}.
 \label{eq:caps-mills}$$ Consequently, $$\frac{(n-1)t}{1-t^2}
 \le-\frac{d}{dt}\log F_n(t)
 \le\frac{n-1}{1-t^2}.
 \label{eq:caps-hazard}$$ These exact inequalities avoid dimension-dependent prefactors in ratios of cap probabilities.

<a id="eq:caps-thresholds"></a>
All the thresholds satisfy $$\tau_i=t_*+O_{a,A,Q}(\log n/n),\qquad
 \tau_i\ge t_*-C_a/n,
 \label{eq:caps-thresholds}$$ for sufficiently large $n$, and hence belong to the fixed interval $J=[t_*/2,(1+t_*)/2]\subset(0,1)$. Since $M=(2F_n(t_*))^{-1}$, we have $c_i=F_n(\tau_i)/F_n(t_*)$. The logarithmic derivative in <a href="#eq:caps-hazard" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:caps-hazard">[eq:caps-hazard]</a> is bounded by $C_an$ on $J$. This proves $c_i\le C_a$, while the lower radial bound proves $c_i\ge n^{-C_{a,A}}$. More explicitly, putting $\delta_i=[1-|x_i|^2/a^2]_+$, elementary algebra gives $$\begin{gathered}
 (\tau_i-t_*)_+\le C_a(\delta_i+Qn^{-4}),\qquad
 (t_*-\tau_i)_+\le C_a(n^{-1}+Qn^{-4}),\\
 n\delta_i\le C_{a,A}\log n+O_{a,Q}(n^{-3}).
\end{gathered}$$ Integrating <a href="#eq:caps-hazard" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:caps-hazard">[eq:caps-hazard]</a> therefore gives $-\log c_i\le C_a(1+n\delta_i)$, after increasing the starting dimension so that $Qn^{-3}\le1$. As $s_i=\max(\log2,-\log c_i)$, this proves <a href="#eq:caps-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:caps-cost">[eq:caps-cost]</a>. The additive constant is needed even when $c_i\ge1/2$.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### The overlap bound.

<a id="eq:caps-bisector"></a>
Consider two ordinary half caps with thresholds $\tau,\upsilon$ drawn from our list and with axis angle $\theta\in[0,\pi]$. Put $t=(\tau+\upsilon)/2$. If $\theta<\pi$, adding their two defining inequalities shows that their intersection lies in the half cap along the angle bisector with threshold $$q=t/\cos(\theta/2).$$ The intersection is empty if $\theta=\pi$ or $q\ge1$, so suppose $q<1$. Let $f(z)=\log(1-z^2)$. Its second derivative is bounded in absolute value on $J$, whence $$f(t)-\tfrac12 f(\tau)-\tfrac12 f(\upsilon)
 \le C_a(\tau-\upsilon)^2.$$ Furthermore, $$\frac{1-q^2}{1-t^2}
 =1-\frac{t^2}{1-t^2}\tan^2(\theta/2)
 \le e^{-c_a'\theta^2},$$ using $t\ge t_*/2$ and $\tan(\theta/2)\ge\theta/2$. Apply the upper bound in <a href="#eq:caps-mills" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:caps-mills">[eq:caps-mills]</a> to $F_n(q)$ and the lower bound to both terms in the following denominator: $$\frac{F_n(q)}{\sqrt{F_n(\tau)F_n(\upsilon)}}
 \le q^{-1}\exp\{C_an(\tau-\upsilon)^2-c_a'n\theta^2\}
 \le C_a e^{-c_a'n\theta^2}.
 \label{eq:caps-bisector}$$ Indeed, $q^{-1}\le2/t_*$, and $n(\tau-\upsilon)^2=O_{a,A,Q}((\log n)^2/n)\to0$ by <a href="#eq:caps-thresholds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:caps-thresholds">[eq:caps-thresholds]</a>. This last fact is the benefit of the thin shell. The argument does not require $q\in J$, so it also applies when the bisector threshold approaches 1.

For projective angle $\phi=\angle(x_i,x_j)$, the four pairs of signed half caps have ordinary angles $\phi$ or $\pi-\phi\ge\phi$. Summing <a href="#eq:caps-bisector" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:caps-bisector">[eq:caps-bisector]</a> over these four disjoint sign choices and using $c_i=2MF_n(\tau_i)$ gives $$\mu(C_i\cap C_j)\le C_a\sqrt{c_ic_j}\,e^{-c_a'n\phi^2}.$$ By <a href="#eq:caps-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:caps-cost">[eq:caps-cost]</a>, $c_i/\bar c_i\le\max(1,2C_a)$, so clipping the denominator costs only a constant. Independently, the trivial intersection bound gives $$\frac{\mu(C_i\cap C_j)}{\sqrt{\bar c_i\bar c_j}}
 \le C_a e^{-|s_i-s_j|/2}.$$ To see this, order the clipped intensities as $\bar c_i\le\bar c_j$. If $\bar c_i<1/2$, use $\mu(C_i\cap C_j)\le c_i=\bar c_i$; otherwise both clipped intensities equal $1/2$, and use $c_i\le C_a$. The minimum of the two bounds is at most their geometric mean. Together with $$d(i,j)^2\le2\bigl(n\phi^2+|s_i-s_j|\bigr),$$ this proves <a href="#eq:caps-affinity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:caps-affinity">[eq:caps-affinity]</a>.

Projective angle satisfies the triangle inequality, as the quotient of spherical distance under antipodal identification. The inequality $\sqrt{x+y}\le\sqrt x+\sqrt y$ shows that $\sqrt{|s_i-s_j|}$ also satisfies it. Thus $d$ is a pseudometric; distinct labels, including repeated caps, may have distance zero.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
###### A common intersection.

Set $r=r(S)$ and $m_S=|S|$, and choose an index attaining the minimum defining $r$. By the triangle inequality, each axis in $S$ has projective angle at most $2\sqrt{r/n}$ from $e_0=e_{i_0}$. Since $F_n$ is strictly decreasing, the raw-minimum anchor has the largest threshold: $\tau_0=\tau_{i_0}\ge\tau_i$. Orient each axis so that its ordinary angle $\phi_i$ with $e_0$ equals its projective angle, and write $$e_i=(\cos\phi_i)e_0+(\sin\phi_i)v_i,\qquad
 \Phi=\max_{i\in S}\phi_i\le2\sqrt{r/n},$$ where $v_i$ is a unit tangent vector. For $\phi_i=0$, its choice is immaterial.

<a id="eq:caps-tangent"></a>
We use the elementary bound $$\mathbb{P}(W\cdot v<-h)\le e^{-dh^2/2}
 \label{eq:caps-tangent}$$ for a uniform $W\in S^{d-1}$, $d\ge2$, any unit $v$, and $h\ge0$. Here is a proof. Rotational invariance reduces to $X=W_1$. Integrating the derivative of $x^{2j-1}(1-x^2)^{(d-1)/2}$ on $[-1,1]$ shows that $$\mathbb{E} X^{2j}
 =\frac{2j-1}{d+2j-2}\mathbb{E} X^{2j-2}
 \le\frac{(2j)!}{2^j j!d^j}.$$ Odd moments vanish. The exponential series therefore gives $\mathbb{E} e^{zX}\le e^{z^2/(2d)}$; applying Markov’s inequality with $z=-dh$ proves <a href="#eq:caps-tangent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:caps-tangent">[eq:caps-tangent]</a>.

Conditionally on $u\cdot e_0=t$, a uniform sphere point has the form $u=te_0+\sqrt{1-t^2}\,W$, with $W$ uniform on the tangent sphere $S^{n-2}$. Put $$h=\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2m_S)}{n-1}},\qquad
 \delta=2\Phi^2+4\Phi h.$$ By <a href="#eq:caps-tangent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:caps-tangent">[eq:caps-tangent]</a> and a union bound, with conditional probability at least $1/2$ one has $W\cdot v_i\ge-h$ for every $i\in S$. For large enough $n$, $\cos\Phi\ge1/2$ and $\tau_0+\delta\in J$, because $r\le B_0\log n$ and $m_S\le n^2$. On that tangent event, whenever $t>\tau_0+\delta$, $$u\cdot e_i
 \ge t\cos\phi_i-h\sin\phi_i
 >\tau_0-\Phi^2/2+\delta/2-h\Phi
 \ge\tau_0\ge\tau_i.$$ Thus all these points lie in every double cap. Integration over the axial coordinate gives $$\mu\left(\bigcap_{i\in S}C_i\right)
 \ge\frac M2F_n(\tau_0+\delta)
 =\frac{c_{i_0}}4\frac{F_n(\tau_0+\delta)}{F_n(\tau_0)}
 \ge\frac{c_{i_0}}4 e^{-C_an\delta},$$ where the last step follows from <a href="#eq:caps-hazard" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:caps-hazard">[eq:caps-hazard]</a> on $J$. Finally, $$n\delta\le C\bigl(r+\sqrt{r\log(2m_S)}\bigr)
 \le C\bigl(r+\log(2m_S)\bigr).$$ Absorb the fixed factors into $L=L(a)$ and use $c_{i_0}\ge\bar c_{i_0}$ to obtain <a href="#eq:caps-lens" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:caps-lens">[eq:caps-lens]</a>. If $r=0$, then $\Phi=\delta=0$, and the same proof applies. ◻

</details>

For the parameters of the construction, the overlap vanishes beyond a fixed angle. We record this separately because zero overlap gives more than the exponentially small bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:caps">4</a>.

<a id="stmt-cor-caps-disjoint"></a>
<div id="cor:caps-disjoint" class="corollary">

**Corollary 5**. *With $\varepsilon=2^{-30}$ and $a=1+\varepsilon$, caps satisfying the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:caps">4</a> are disjoint whenever their axes have projective angle greater than $4\sqrt\varepsilon$, for all sufficiently large $n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-caps-disjoint"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-caps-disjoint</summary>

*Proof.* Each half cap has angular radius $\arccos\tau_i$. Since $$\cos(2\sqrt\varepsilon)
 \le1-2\varepsilon+\tfrac23\varepsilon^2
 <1-\varepsilon\le\frac1{1+\varepsilon},$$ the estimate $\tau_i\ge1/a-C_a/n$ shows that its radius is strictly less than $2\sqrt\varepsilon$ for large $n$. Every pair of signed axes is at ordinary angle at least the projective angle. The spherical triangle inequality therefore excludes a point in any pair of half caps at projective separation greater than $4\sqrt\varepsilon$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:blocks"></a>
### Grouping cap conditions

We next group the cap conditions into blocks and choose a measurable set inside each common intersection. A point in this set meets every cap condition in its block. We need a lower bound on the product of the chosen measures and control of their mutual overlaps. Together these bounds will control the self-overlap of the Poisson witnesses in Section <a href="#sec:poisson" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:poisson">6</a>. The merger construction and the weighted row estimate follow Li–Liu (Li and Liu 2026, Proposition 9.1 and Lemmas 9.4–9.5) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[LiLiu2026Lattice](#ref-LiLiu2026Lattice)]. We give the argument for arbitrary indexed lists, retaining repeated members.

<a id="stmt-prop-blocks"></a>
<div id="prop:blocks" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:block-affinity-input"></a>
<a id="eq:block-lens-input"></a>
<a id="eq:block-anchor-loss"></a>
<a id="eq:block-log-cost"></a>
<a id="eq:block-first-budget"></a>
<a id="eq:block-row-budget"></a>
**Proposition 6** (Block estimates). *Fix constants $C_0\ge1$, $c_0>0$, and $L\ge1$. There are constants $B_0,K,C\ge1$ and $n_1$, depending only on these three constants, with the following property. Let $n\ge n_1$, let $(X,\mu)$ be an atomless finite measure space, and let $(C_i)_{i\in I}$ be an indexed list of measurable sets with $0<c_i:=\mu(C_i)<\infty$ and $1\le m:=|I|\le n^2$. Set $$\bar c_i=\min(c_i,1/2),\qquad s_i=-\log\bar c_i,$$ and let $d$ be a pseudometric on $I$. For nonempty $S\subseteq I$, write $$r(S)=\min_{j\in S}\max_{i\in S}d(i,j)^2,$$ and choose an index $i(S)\in S$ minimizing the uncapped measure $c_i$. Assume that $$\label{eq:block-affinity-input}
 \frac{\mu(C_i\cap C_j)}{\sqrt{\bar c_i\bar c_j}}
 \le C_0e^{-c_0d(i,j)^2}\qquad(i,j\in I),$$ and that, whenever $r(S)\le B_0\log n$, $$\label{eq:block-lens-input}
 \mu\left(\bigcap_{i\in S}C_i\right)
 \ge e^{-L(1+r(S)+\log|S|)}\bar c_{i(S)}.$$ Then there is a partition $\mathcal P$ of $I$ and measurable sets $E_S\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in S}C_i$, $S\in\mathcal P$, of measures $\lambda_S=\mu(E_S)$ such that $$\label{eq:block-anchor-loss}
 n^{-K}\bar c_{i(S)}\le\lambda_S\le\frac12.$$ Writing $q_S=-\log\lambda_S$ and $$w_{ST}=
 \frac{\mu(E_S\cap E_T)}
 {\sqrt{\lambda_S(1+\lambda_S)\lambda_T(1+\lambda_T)}},$$ we have $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{S\in\mathcal P}q_S
 &\le\sum_{i\in I}s_i+\frac{m}{10},
 \label{eq:block-log-cost}\\
 \sum_{S\in\mathcal P}\log(1+\lambda_S^{-1})
 &\le C\left(m+\sum_{i\in I}s_i\right),
 \label{eq:block-first-budget}\\
 \sum_{S\in\mathcal P}
 \log\left(1+\sum_{T\in\mathcal P\setminus\{S\}}w_{ST}\right)
 &\le C\left(m+\sum_{i\in I}s_i\right).
 \label{eq:block-row-budget}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-blocks"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-blocks</summary>

*Proof.* Set $\zeta=1/10$ and $R_*=B_0\log n$; we will choose $B_0$ after the constants governing the row estimate. Define $$\ell(S)=
 \begin{cases}
 0,&|S|=1,\\
 L(1+r(S)+\log|S|),&|S|\ge2.
 \end{cases}$$ For each final block $S$, we will choose a common subset of intensity $e^{-\ell(S)}\bar c_{i(S)}$. Thus $\ell(S)$ is the logarithmic loss assigned to that block. The merger condition below keeps the total loss at most $\zeta m$, while terminality will control the number of nearby blocks with small loss.

<a id="eq:block-merge"></a>
<a id="eq:block-terminal-properties"></a>
*Constructing the partition.* Start with the singleton partition. Whenever a subfamily of at least two current blocks has union $W$ satisfying $$\label{eq:block-merge}
 r(W)\le R_*,\qquad \ell(W)\le\zeta|W|,$$ replace that entire subfamily by $W$. The subfamily may have any size. Each merger decreases the number of blocks, so this procedure terminates after at most $m-1$ mergers. Every resulting block satisfies $$\label{eq:block-terminal-properties}
 r(S)\le R_*,\qquad \ell(S)\le\zeta|S|,$$ and no subfamily of at least two terminal blocks satisfies <a href="#eq:block-merge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-merge">[eq:block-merge]</a>.

<a id="eq:block-anchor-radius"></a>
<a id="eq:block-chosen-intensity"></a>
The anchor $i(S)$ need not attain the minimum defining $r(S)$. Applying the triangle inequality through an index that does attain it gives $$\label{eq:block-anchor-radius}
 d(i,i(S))\le2\sqrt{r(S)}\le2\sqrt{\ell(S)/L}
 \qquad(i\in S).$$ For singletons this holds with both sides zero. By <a href="#eq:block-lens-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-lens-input">[eq:block-lens-input]</a> and atomlessness, choose $E_S\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in S}C_i$ of exactly the measure $$\label{eq:block-chosen-intensity}
 \lambda_S=e^{-\ell(S)}\bar c_{i(S)}.$$ For singletons this choice is possible because $\bar c_i\le c_i$. Thus $q_S=s_{i(S)}+\ell(S)\ge\log2$. Different blocks have different anchor labels, and hence $$\sum_Sq_S
 \le\sum_i s_i+\sum_S\zeta|S|
 =\sum_i s_i+\zeta m.$$ This proves <a href="#eq:block-log-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-log-cost">[eq:block-log-cost]</a>. For $n\ge3$, <a href="#eq:block-terminal-properties" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-terminal-properties">[eq:block-terminal-properties]</a> and $m\le n^2$ give $$\ell(S)\le L(1+R_*+\log m)\le L(B_0+3)\log n.$$ Consequently <a href="#eq:block-anchor-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-anchor-loss">[eq:block-anchor-loss]</a> holds with $K=L(B_0+3)$. Also $$\log(1+\lambda_S^{-1})
 =q_S+\log(1+e^{-q_S})
 \le q_S+\log(3/2)
 \le\left(1+\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log2}\right)q_S,$$ which proves <a href="#eq:block-first-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-first-budget">[eq:block-first-budget]</a>. It remains to control the overlap rows.

*What terminality implies.* We claim that a constant $C_{\mathrm{ct}}=C_{\mathrm{ct}}(L,\zeta)$ bounds the number of blocks satisfying <a id="eq:block-count-conditions"></a>$$\label{eq:block-count-conditions}
 d(z,i(S))^2\le t,\qquad \ell(S)\le u$$ by $C_{\mathrm{ct}}(1+t+u)$, provided that $z\in I$, $t,u\ge0$, and <a id="eq:block-count-radius"></a>$$\label{eq:block-count-radius}
 8t+8u/L\le R_*.$$ This is a count of blocks, including blocks with coinciding anchors in pseudometric distance.

Let $h$ be the number of such blocks and let $W$ be their union, of size $N$. If $h\le1$ there is nothing to prove. Otherwise choose one of their anchors, say $i(S_0)$. For every $i\in S\subseteq W$, <a href="#eq:block-anchor-radius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-anchor-radius">[eq:block-anchor-radius]</a> gives $$d(i(S_0),i)
 \le d(i(S_0),z)+d(z,i(S))+d(i(S),i)
 \le2\sqrt t+2\sqrt{u/L}.$$ As $i(S_0)\in W$, it follows that $$r(W)\le8t+8u/L=:D_*\le R_*.$$ Choose a fixed integer $N_0\ge2$ so large that $L\log N\le\zeta N/2$ for all $N\ge N_0$. If $$h\ge N_0\quad\text{and}\quad
 h\ge\frac{2L}{\zeta}(1+D_*),$$ then $N\ge h$ implies $$\ell(W)\le L(1+D_*+\log N)\le\zeta N.$$ The whole subfamily could then be merged, contradicting terminality. Since $1+D_*\le8(1+t+u)$, the claim follows, for example with $C_{\mathrm{ct}}=1+N_0+16L/\zeta$. Here the logarithm being absorbed is $\log N$, the logarithm of the number of original labels in the union; the block sizes need not be comparable.

<a id="eq:block-weight-trivial"></a>
<a id="eq:block-weight-affinity"></a>
*Estimating a row.* The elementary intersection bound and <a href="#eq:block-affinity-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-affinity-input">[eq:block-affinity-input]</a> give, respectively, $$\begin{aligned}
 w_{ST}&\le e^{-|q_S-q_T|/2}\le1,
 \label{eq:block-weight-trivial}\\
 w_{ST}&\le C_0\exp\left\{
   \frac{\ell(S)+\ell(T)}2-c_0d(i(S),i(T))^2\right\}.
 \label{eq:block-weight-affinity}
\end{aligned}$$ For the first inequality, use $\mu(E_S\cap E_T)\le\min(\lambda_S,\lambda_T)$ and discard the factors $1+\lambda_S,1+\lambda_T$ in the denominator. For the second, use $E_S\subseteq C_{i(S)}$ and <a href="#eq:block-chosen-intensity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-chosen-intensity">[eq:block-chosen-intensity]</a>.

<a id="eq:block-bin-count"></a>
Fix $S$. For $k\ge0$, put in the $k$th bin those $T\ne S$ for which $e^{-(k+1)}<w_{ST}\le e^{-k}$; zero weights can be omitted. Writing $Q=q_S+k+1\ge1$, the two bounds above imply <a id="eq:block-bin-parameters"></a>$$\label{eq:block-bin-parameters}
 \ell(T)\le q_T<q_S+2(k+1)\le2Q,
 \qquad
 d(i(S),i(T))^2\le A_1Q,$$ where $A_1=(\log C_0+2)/c_0$. Indeed, taking logarithms in <a href="#eq:block-weight-affinity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-weight-affinity">[eq:block-weight-affinity]</a> gives $$c_0d(i(S),i(T))^2
 <\log C_0+\frac{q_S+q_T}{2}+k+1
 \le\log C_0+q_S+2(k+1)
 \le(\log C_0+2)Q.$$ Now set $$A_2=\max\{1,8A_1+16/L\},\qquad \delta=A_2^{-1}.$$ If $Q\le\delta R_*$, apply the terminality count with $z=i(S)$, $t=A_1Q$, and $u=2Q$. The number of blocks in the $k$th bin is at most $$\label{eq:block-bin-count}
 C_{\mathrm{ct}}(1+A_1Q+2Q)\le C_1(q_S+k+1),$$ where $C_1=C_{\mathrm{ct}}(A_1+3)$. These constants are independent of $B_0$. Choose $B_0\ge\max\{1,6/\delta\}$, and increase $n_1$ so that $R_*\ge4/\delta$ for $n\ge n_1$.

<a id="eq:block-row-cost"></a>
First suppose $q_S\le\delta R_*/4$, and put $J=\lfloor\delta R_*/2\rfloor$. For $0\le k\le J$ we have $q_S+k+1\le\delta R_*$, so <a href="#eq:block-bin-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-bin-count">[eq:block-bin-count]</a> bounds the total weight in these bins by $$C_1\sum_{k=0}^J e^{-k}(q_S+k+1)\le C_2(1+q_S).$$ Every remaining weight is at most $e^{-(J+1)}\le e^{-\delta R_*/2}$, and there are at most $n^2$ of them. Their sum is therefore at most $$n^2e^{-\delta R_*/2}=n^{2-\delta B_0/2}\le1.$$ Since $q_S\ge\log2$, this proves $$\label{eq:block-row-cost}
 \log\left(1+\sum_{T\ne S}w_{ST}\right)\le C_3q_S$$ in the present case. If $q_S>\delta R_*/4$, the simpler bound <a href="#eq:block-weight-trivial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-weight-trivial">[eq:block-weight-trivial]</a> gives $$\log\left(1+\sum_{T\ne S}w_{ST}\right)
 \le\log(1+n^2)\le3\log n
 <\frac{12}{\delta B_0}q_S.$$ Thus <a href="#eq:block-row-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-row-cost">[eq:block-row-cost]</a> holds for every block after enlarging $C_3$. Summing it and applying <a href="#eq:block-log-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-log-cost">[eq:block-log-cost]</a> proves <a href="#eq:block-row-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-row-budget">[eq:block-row-budget]</a>. ◻

</details>

Lemma <a href="#lem:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:caps">4</a> supplies the two hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:blocks">6</a> for the residual caps in the thin shell. Its constants $C_0,c_0,L$ are independent of the fixed radius multiplier $B_0$, so one first chooses these constants, then the value of $B_0$ specified in the proof, and finally the dimension threshold required by Lemma <a href="#lem:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:caps">4</a>. All constructions use the indexed list alone. In particular, equal caps remain separate labels, and for any fixed list the partition and the measurable subsets may be chosen before the Poisson process is sampled.

<a id="sec:poisson"></a>
### Poisson witnesses and their lower tail

The blocks constructed in Proposition <a href="#prop:blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:blocks">6</a> give measurable sets of slab normals, one set for each block of residuals. Choosing a distinct Poisson point from each set certifies that all the residuals are cut. We now estimate the probability that none of a finite collection of targets has such a certificate. The lower-tail inequality originates in the work of Janson (Janson 1990) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Janson1990LargeDeviations](#ref-Janson1990LargeDeviations)]. The continuous version and diagram method below follow Li–Liu (Li and Liu 2026, Lemma 6.4, Equation (6.16), and Lemma 9.6) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[LiLiu2026Lattice](#ref-LiLiu2026Lattice)]. We include the finite inequality, its passage to measurable slots, and the matching estimates to specify all multiplicities and diagonal terms.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### The finite inequality

The events in the finite approximation are indexed by ordered choices of cells. Different choices can use the same set of cells, so repeated underlying subsets must remain separate indices.

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-janson"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-janson" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:finite-janson"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Finite indexed Janson inequality). *Let $(X_v)_{v\in V}$ be independent Bernoulli variables on a finite set $V$, and let $(H_i)_{i=1}^N$ be an indexed family of nonempty subsets of $V$, with repetitions allowed. Set $$I_i=\prod_{v\in H_i}X_v,\qquad Y=\sum_{i=1}^NI_i,\qquad m=\mathbb{E}Y,
 \qquad
 \Delta^*=\sum_{\substack{1\le i,j\le N\\H_i\cap H_j\ne\varnothing}}
                  \mathbb{E}(I_iI_j).$$ If $m>0$, then $\Delta^*\ge m$ and $$\mathbb{P}(Y=0)\le \exp\left(-\frac{m^2}{2\Delta^*}\right).
 \label{eq:finite-janson}$$ The dependency sum is ordered and includes the diagonal.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-janson"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-janson</summary>

<a id="eq:harris-opposite"></a>
*Proof.* We first recall Harris’s correlation inequality for product Bernoulli measures (Harris 1960) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Harris1960Correlation](#ref-Harris1960Correlation)], together with its elementary proof. Increasing functions of independent Bernoulli variables have nonnegative covariance. Indeed, induction on the number of variables and conditioning on the last variable reduce the additional covariance term to $p(1-p)(f_1-f_0)(g_1-g_0)\ge0$, where $f_b,g_b$ are the corresponding conditional means. Thus an increasing event $B$ and a decreasing event $F$ satisfy $$\mathbb{P}(B\mid F)\le\mathbb{P}(B)
 \quad\text{whenever }\mathbb{P}(F)>0.
 \label{eq:harris-opposite}$$

<a id="eq:janson-first"></a>
Write $B_i=\{I_i=1\}$, $p_i=\mathbb{P}(B_i)$, and $p_{ij}=\mathbb{P}(B_i\cap B_j)$. For each $i$, let $F_i$ be the event that none of the earlier $B_j$ with $H_i\cap H_j=\varnothing$ occurs, and let $G_i$ exclude the remaining earlier events. If some earlier avoidance event has probability zero, the conclusion is immediate. Otherwise all the conditional probabilities below are defined. Since $F_i$ uses only coordinates outside $H_i$, it is independent of $B_i$. It is also decreasing. A conditional union bound and <a href="#eq:harris-opposite" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:harris-opposite">[eq:harris-opposite]</a> therefore give $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb{P}(B_i\mid F_i\cap G_i)
 &\ge\mathbb{P}(B_i\cap G_i\mid F_i)\\
 &\ge p_i-
       \sum_{\substack{j<i\\H_i\cap H_j\ne\varnothing}}p_{ij}.
 \end{aligned}$$ The first inequality uses $\mathbb{P}(G_i\mid F_i)\le1$. Multiplying the successive avoidance probabilities and using $1-x\le e^{-x}$ yields $$\mathbb{P}(Y=0)\le\exp(-m+\Delta_{\mathrm{off}}/2),
 \qquad
 \Delta_{\mathrm{off}}=
 \sum_{\substack{i\ne j\\H_i\cap H_j\ne\varnothing}}p_{ij}.
 \label{eq:janson-first}$$ This remains valid when an intermediate lower bound above is negative.

Attach to each index $i$ a private independent Bernoulli variable of parameter $q$, and require it as part of $B_i$. The new mean is $qm$, and its ordered off-diagonal sum is $q^2\Delta_{\mathrm{off}}$. The original zero event implies the new zero event, so <a href="#eq:janson-first" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:janson-first">[eq:janson-first]</a> bounds its probability by $\exp(-qm+q^2\Delta_{\mathrm{off}}/2)$. Since $\Delta^*=m+\Delta_{\mathrm{off}}$, choosing $q=m/\Delta^*\in(0,1]$ proves <a href="#eq:finite-janson" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-janson">[eq:finite-janson]</a>. The proof retains every index even when two supports coincide. ◻

</details>

Janson–Warnke (Janson and Warnke 2016, Equation (2)) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[JansonWarnke2016LowerTail](#ref-JansonWarnke2016LowerTail)] record a stronger constant in this indexed formulation. The constant in Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-janson" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-janson">7</a> suffices here.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Measurable slots and matching diagrams

Let $(\Omega,\mu)$ be a finite nonatomic standard Borel measure space, and let $\Pi$ be a Poisson process of intensity $\mu$. Consider a finite nonempty family of witness types indexed by $\alpha\in\mathcal A$. In the covering application, a type is a target point and its slots are blocks of residuals. For each $\alpha$, let $I_\alpha$ be a finite nonempty set of labeled slots, with measurable sets $E_{\alpha i}\subseteq\Omega$ satisfying $\lambda_{\alpha i}:=\mu(E_{\alpha i})>0$. Define its *activity* by $$\nu_\alpha=\prod_{i\in I_\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha i},$$ and let $Z_\alpha$ count injective assignments of Poisson points to the slots, with the point assigned to $i$ lying in $E_{\alpha i}$. These are ordered counts: equal slot sets do not identify their labels.

<a id="eq:diagram-definition"></a>
A *partial matching* between $I_\alpha$ and $I_\beta$ is a set of pairs $(i,j)$ with no repeated first or second coordinate. Put $$D_{\alpha\beta}=
 \nu_\alpha\nu_\beta
 \sum_{\substack{M\text{ a partial matching}\\M\ne\varnothing}}
 \prod_{(i,j)\in M}
 \frac{\mu(E_{\alpha i}\cap E_{\beta j})}
      {\lambda_{\alpha i}\lambda_{\beta j}}.
 \label{eq:diagram-definition}$$ The Poisson factorial-moment formula gives $$\mathbb E Z_\alpha=\nu_\alpha,\qquad
 \mathbb E[Z_\alpha Z_\beta]
       =\nu_\alpha\nu_\beta+D_{\alpha\beta}.$$ For the second identity, group pairs of injective assignments by the partial matching of slots occupied by the same point. The empty matching contributes $\nu_\alpha\nu_\beta$; the nonempty matchings give exactly the terms in <a href="#eq:diagram-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diagram-definition">[eq:diagram-definition]</a>. Thus $D_{\alpha\beta}$ is the covariance of $Z_\alpha$ and $Z_\beta$.

<a id="stmt-prop-poisson"></a>
<div id="prop:poisson" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:poisson-janson"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Poisson lower tail). *Let $\Pi$ be a Poisson process on a finite nonatomic standard Borel measure space. For each member of a finite nonempty family, take a finite nonempty labeled list of measurable slots of positive intensity. Let $Z_\alpha$ count their injective assignments, let $\nu_\alpha$ be the product of their intensities, and define $D_{\alpha\beta}$ by <a href="#eq:diagram-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diagram-definition">[eq:diagram-definition]</a>. Then $\mathbb{E}Z_\alpha=\nu_\alpha$, and $$\mathbb{P}(Z_\alpha=0\text{ for all }\alpha\in\mathcal A)
 \le\exp\left[-\frac{(\sum_\alpha\nu_\alpha)^2}
                        {2\sum_{\alpha,\beta}D_{\alpha\beta}}\right].
 \label{eq:poisson-janson}$$ The denominator is positive: the full identity matching in $D_{\alpha\alpha}$ contributes $\nu_\alpha$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-poisson"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-poisson</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\tau=\mu(\Omega)>0$. Conditional on $N=\Pi(\Omega)$, the points are independent with law $\mu/\tau$ and are almost surely distinct. If $k=|I_\alpha|$, the Poisson factorial moment $\mathbb{E}(N)_k=\tau^k$ gives $$\mathbb{E}Z_\alpha
 =\mathbb{E}(N)_k\,\tau^{-k}\prod_i\lambda_{\alpha i}
 =\nu_\alpha.$$ In particular, no factorial divides the activity, even for repeated slot sets.

<a id="eq:cell-occupation-error"></a>
We apply Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-janson" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-janson">7</a> to cell-occupation indicators. Fix $\delta>0$. Form the Boolean atoms generated by all the slot sets and subdivide each positive-measure atom into finitely many measurable cells of measure at most $\delta$, using nonatomicity. After omitting null cells, this gives a finite partition $\mathcal P_\delta$ that respects every slot. Modifications on the common null set do not affect the Poisson process. For $C\in\mathcal P_\delta$, set $$a_C=\mu(C),\qquad X_C=\mathbf1_{\{\Pi(C)>0\}},\qquad
 p_C=\mathbb{E}X_C=1-e^{-a_C}.$$ These variables are independent and $$0\le a_C-p_C\le\tfrac12a_C^2\le\tfrac\delta2a_C.
 \label{eq:cell-occupation-error}$$

For each $\alpha$, retain every injective assignment of cells $f:I_\alpha\to\mathcal P_\delta$ with $f(i)\subseteq E_{\alpha i}$. Its indicator is $\prod_iX_{f(i)}$. Let $Y_\delta$ be the sum over these indexed assignments. Different assignments remain different indices even when their underlying cell sets agree.

<a id="eq:cell-collision-error"></a>
<a id="eq:cell-mean-limit"></a>
The following estimate controls all cell collisions. For a fixed list $F_1,\ldots,F_r$ of unions of cells, let $Q_\delta(F_1,\ldots,F_r)$ be the sum of $\prod_{v=1}^rp_{C_v}$ over distinct cells $C_v\subseteq F_v$. Then $$\begin{split}
 0\le\prod_v\mu(F_v)-Q_\delta(F_1,\ldots,F_r)
 \le\delta\bigg[&\frac r2\prod_v\mu(F_v)\\
 &+\sum_{v<w}\mu(F_v\cap F_w)
                      \prod_{u\ne v,w}\mu(F_u)\bigg].
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:cell-collision-error}$$ Indeed, the unconstrained sum factors over the slots, and <a href="#eq:cell-occupation-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cell-occupation-error">[eq:cell-occupation-error]</a>, followed by telescoping the product, bounds its difference from $\prod_v\mu(F_v)$ by the first term. The weight of assignments with $C_v=C_w$ is at most $$\sum_{C\subseteq F_v\cap F_w}p_C^2
       \prod_{u\ne v,w}\mu(F_u)
 \le\delta\mu(F_v\cap F_w)\prod_{u\ne v,w}\mu(F_u).$$ A union bound proves <a href="#eq:cell-collision-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cell-collision-error">[eq:cell-collision-error]</a>. Empty products are 1, and the estimate also holds if a set has measure zero. Applying it to each slot list gives $$m_\delta:=\mathbb{E}Y_\delta\longrightarrow\sum_\alpha\nu_\alpha.
 \label{eq:cell-mean-limit}$$

<a id="eq:cell-diagram-limit"></a>
It remains to identify the finite dependency sum and the limiting event. For two internally injective assignments $f$ and $g$, the complete set of equalities $f(i)=g(j)$ is a unique partial matching $M$. Their Bernoulli supports intersect precisely when $M\ne\varnothing$. For fixed $\alpha,\beta,M$, identify the slots paired by $M$ and leave the other slots as singletons. Assign to each resulting class its slot set, or the intersection of its two slot sets. Denote these sets by $(F_v)_v$. Requiring distinct cells for these classes excludes exactly the unintended equalities. Thus the contribution with exact matching $M$ is $Q_\delta((F_v)_v)$, and its limit is $$\prod_v\mu(F_v)=\nu_\alpha\nu_\beta
 \prod_{(i,j)\in M}
 \frac{\mu(E_{\alpha i}\cap E_{\beta j})}
      {\lambda_{\alpha i}\lambda_{\beta j}}.$$ All sums are finite. Estimate <a href="#eq:cell-collision-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cell-collision-error">[eq:cell-collision-error]</a> therefore shows that the ordered dependency sum, including identical assignment pairs, satisfies $$\Delta^*_\delta\longrightarrow\sum_{\alpha,\beta}D_{\alpha\beta}.
 \label{eq:cell-diagram-limit}$$ Identical assignments give the full identity matching; other full matchings caused by repeated slots remain separate terms.

Finally, couple the cell variables with the original process. The probability that any cell contains two or more points is at most $$\frac12\sum_C\mathbb{E}[\Pi(C)(\Pi(C)-1)]
 =\frac12\sum_Ca_C^2\le\frac{\delta\tau}{2}.$$ Outside this event, point injections and occupied-cell injections are in bijection, simultaneously for every witness type. Hence $$\left|\mathbb{P}(Y_\delta=0)-
       \mathbb{P}(Z_\alpha=0\text{ for all }\alpha)\right|
 \le\frac{\delta\tau}{2}.$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-janson" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-janson">7</a> and let $\delta\downarrow0$, using <a href="#eq:cell-mean-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cell-mean-limit">[eq:cell-mean-limit]</a> and <a href="#eq:cell-diagram-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cell-diagram-limit">[eq:cell-diagram-limit]</a>. The identity-matching contribution makes the limiting denominator positive. This proves <a href="#eq:poisson-janson" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:poisson-janson">[eq:poisson-janson]</a>. ◻

</details>

The approximation is performed for each fixed finite family. Its size and the total intensity may grow with dimension; no uniform estimate in those quantities is needed before taking the cell limit. An empty slot list has one empty injection and is treated as a deterministic certificate before applying Proposition <a href="#prop:poisson" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:poisson">8</a>.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Self and cross diagrams

The two budgets in Proposition <a href="#prop:blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:blocks">6</a> now control the self-diagram sum. The factor $1+\lambda_i^{-1}$ pays for identifying the same slot in both copies of a witness.

<a id="stmt-lem-diagrams"></a>
<div id="lem:diagrams" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:self-diagram-product"></a>
<a id="eq:cross-diagram-product"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Matching bounds). *For positive-measure slot lists and the diagrams in <a href="#eq:diagram-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diagram-definition">[eq:diagram-definition]</a>, the following bounds hold. For one witness type, suppress its label and set $$\delta_i=1+\lambda_i^{-1},\qquad
 w_{ij}=\frac{\mu(E_i\cap E_j)}
 {\sqrt{\lambda_i(1+\lambda_i)\lambda_j(1+\lambda_j)}}
 \quad(i\ne j).$$ Then $$1+\frac{D_{\alpha\alpha}}{\nu_\alpha^2}
 \le\prod_i\delta_i\prod_i\left(1+\sum_{j\ne i}w_{ij}\right).
 \label{eq:self-diagram-product}$$ For two witness types, put $$\rho_{\alpha\beta}=
 \sum_{i\in I_\alpha,j\in I_\beta}
 \frac{\mu(E_{\alpha i}\cap E_{\beta j})}
      {\lambda_{\alpha i}\lambda_{\beta j}}.$$ Then $$D_{\alpha\beta}\le\nu_\alpha\nu_\beta
                         (e^{\rho_{\alpha\beta}}-1).
 \label{eq:cross-diagram-product}$$ In particular, if every cross-slot intersection has measure zero, then $D_{\alpha\beta}=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-diagrams"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-diagrams</summary>

*Proof.* Write $h_{ij}=\mu(E_i\cap E_j)/(\lambda_i\lambda_j)$. For the self sum, fix the off-diagonal edges $P$ of a partial matching between two copies of the slot list. Regard $P$ as a directed graph on the common label set. Every vertex has total degree $d_P(i)\in\{0,1,2\}$, since its in-degree and out-degree are at most one. A diagonal loop is available exactly at the vertices of degree zero. Since $h_{ii}=\lambda_i^{-1}$, summing the compatible loop choices gives $$\left(\prod_i\delta_i\right)
 \left(\prod_{(i,j)\in P}h_{ij}\right)
 \left(\prod_{i:d_P(i)>0}\delta_i^{-1}\right)
 \le\left(\prod_i\delta_i\right)\prod_{(i,j)\in P}w_{ij}.$$ Here $\delta_i^{-1}\le \delta_i^{-d_P(i)/2}$ at each incident vertex. This includes directed paths and cycles; Figure <a href="#fig:self-matching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:self-matching">2</a> illustrates the permitted incidences. Summing over $P$ and dropping the requirement of distinct right endpoints bounds the remaining sum by $\prod_i(1+\sum_{j\ne i}w_{ij})$. The empty matching contributes 1, proving <a href="#eq:self-diagram-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:self-diagram-product">[eq:self-diagram-product]</a>.

For two lists, drop all matching restrictions and sum over nonempty subsets of cross edges. With the analogous nonnegative weights $h_{ij}$, the result is at most $\prod_{i,j}(1+h_{ij})-1\le e^{\sum_{i,j}h_{ij}}-1$. This proves <a href="#eq:cross-diagram-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cross-diagram-product">[eq:cross-diagram-product]</a>. ◻

</details>

<figure id="fig:self-matching">

<figcaption>A self-matching after identifying the two copies of the slot labels. The solid edges form the off-diagonal matching $P = {(1,2), (2,3), (4,5), (5,4)}$; each label has at most one incoming and one outgoing edge. The degree $d_{P}(i)$ counts both. A diagonal match is available only at an isolated label, as illustrated by the optional dashed loop at $6$; even the degree-one path endpoints cannot receive a diagonal match.</figcaption>
</figure>
