<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Application to residual caps

We return to the fixed parameters and the spherical intensity $\mu$ of Section <a href="#sec:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:construction">2</a>. The following consequence collects the inputs needed for the target selection in the next section.

<a id="stmt-cor-witnesses"></a>
<div id="cor:witnesses" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:witness-self"></a>
<a id="eq:witness-cross"></a>
**Corollary 10** (Witnesses for residual lists). *Fix $A_0>0$. There are constants $A,C_*,H>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $n$, the following holds. Let $R\ge1$, and let $(x_{\alpha i})_{i=1}^{m_\alpha}$, $\alpha\in\mathcal A$, be any finite nonempty family of deterministic lists satisfying $$1\le m_\alpha\le n^2,\qquad
 r_--3\eta\le |x_{\alpha i}|\le b+3\eta.$$ Define $$C_{\alpha i}=\{u\in S^{n-1}:|u\cdot x_{\alpha i}|>1+3\eta\},
 \qquad
 s_{\alpha i}=-\log\min\{\mu(C_{\alpha i}),1/2\},$$ and suppose $m_\alpha+\sum_i s_{\alpha i}\le A_0R$. For each list there is a nonempty block partition and measurable slots $E_{\alpha S}\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in S}C_{\alpha i}$, chosen independently of $\Pi$, with positive intensities at most $1/2$. Their activities and diagrams satisfy $$e^{-AR}\le\nu_\alpha\le1,\qquad
 D_{\alpha\alpha}\le\nu_\alpha^2e^{C_*R}.
 \label{eq:witness-self}$$ For distinct $\alpha,\beta$, if all cross-residual angles exceed $H\sqrt{\log n/n}$, then $$D_{\alpha\beta}\le n^{-3}\nu_\alpha\nu_\beta.
 \label{eq:witness-cross}$$ If all those angles exceed $4\sqrt\varepsilon$, then $D_{\alpha\beta}=0$. A positive injection count for a list implies that every cap in that list is hit by $\Pi$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-witnesses"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-witnesses</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:caps">4</a> with shell parameter $10$ and perturbation bound $3$. Its affinity and common-intersection estimates supply the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:blocks">6</a>; the common-intersection constant is available before the radius multiplier in that proposition is fixed. Apply the proposition separately to each list, making all choices before sampling $\Pi$. The budget <a href="#eq:block-first-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-first-budget">[eq:block-first-budget]</a> gives $$-\log\nu_\alpha
 \le\sum_S\log(1+\lambda_{\alpha S}^{-1})\le AR.$$ Together with <a href="#eq:block-row-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-row-budget">[eq:block-row-budget]</a> and <a href="#eq:self-diagram-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:self-diagram-product">[eq:self-diagram-product]</a>, it also gives the self bound in <a href="#eq:witness-self" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:witness-self">[eq:witness-self]</a>, after choosing $C_*$.

To check the cross estimate, write $\bar c_{\alpha i}=\min\{\mu(C_{\alpha i}),1/2\}$ and let $i(S)$ be the anchor of a block. Lemma <a href="#lem:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:caps">4</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:blocks">6</a> provide fixed exponents $P_0,P_1>0$ such that $$\bar c_{\alpha i}\ge n^{-P_0},\qquad
 \lambda_{\alpha S}\ge n^{-P_1}\bar c_{\alpha,i(S)},\qquad
 E_{\alpha S}\subseteq C_{\alpha,i(S)}.$$ Apply the affinity estimate to each two-element list of anchor residuals; this does not require the union of the two full lists to have at most $n^2$ entries. It gives $$\frac{\mu(E_{\alpha S}\cap E_{\beta T})}
      {\lambda_{\alpha S}\lambda_{\beta T}}
 \le Cn^{2P_1+P_0}
       e^{-c n\angle(x_{\alpha,i(S)},x_{\beta,i(T)})^2}.$$ There are at most $n^4$ block pairs. Choose $H$ after these constants, so that $cH^2>9+2P_1+P_0$. For sufficiently large $n$, the angular hypothesis gives $\rho_{\alpha\beta}\le n^{-4}$. Equation <a href="#eq:cross-diagram-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cross-diagram-product">[eq:cross-diagram-product]</a> now gives $D_{\alpha\beta}\le2n^{-4}\nu_\alpha\nu_\beta$, which implies <a href="#eq:witness-cross" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:witness-cross">[eq:witness-cross]</a>. At cross angles greater than $4\sqrt\varepsilon$, Corollary <a href="#cor:caps-disjoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:caps-disjoint">5</a> makes every cross-slot intersection empty, and the diagram is zero.

Finally, the point assigned to a block lies in every cap belonging to that block. A positive injection count thus hits every residual cap. ◻

</details>

For a finite center list $(p_i)$ and a target $y$, take all residuals $x_i=y-p_i$ with $|x_i|\le b+3\eta$. If this list satisfies the hypotheses of Corollary <a href="#cor:witnesses" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:witnesses">10</a>, a positive witness excludes each such residual from $K^+$ by a strict slab inequality. All remaining residuals are excluded by its outer ball. Consequently <a id="eq:witness-hole"></a>$$Z_y>0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 y\notin\bigcup_i(p_i+K^+).
 \label{eq:witness-hole}$$ If the relevant list is empty, the target is already a hole. Proposition <a href="#prop:poisson" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:poisson">8</a> supplies an exponential bound on the probability that every chosen target lacks a witness. A second-moment bound alone would not give the decay required for the finite-pattern union in the final argument.

<a id="sec:targets"></a>
### Many targets for each finite pattern

We now fix one of the rounded center lists supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:localization">3</a>. Our goal is to bound the probability that this list covers the window $B_D$ by translates of $K^+$. The good subset of the window contains many points whose residual vectors are separated at a small angular scale. Only a small fraction of pairs need fail a larger, fixed separation. The former property controls every cross diagram; the latter makes most cross diagrams vanish.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### A deterministic choice of targets

The following volume estimate controls projective angles, including both possible orientations of a residual vector.

<a id="stmt-lem-cylinder"></a>
<div id="lem:cylinder" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Cylinder estimate). *Let $n\ge2$, let $D,L>0$, and let $G\subset B_D$ be measurable with $|G|\ge\omega_nD^n/4$. Let $Y,Z$ be independent uniform points of $G$, and let $p,q\in\mathbb R^n$. For $0<\xi<\pi/2$, <a id="eq:cylinder"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
&\mathbb P\bigl(0<|Y-p|\le L,\ 0<|Z-q|\le L,
                 \ \angle(Y-p,Z-q)\le\xi\bigr)
\notag\\[-2pt]
&\hspace{30mm}\le
 8\frac{\omega_{n-1}}{\omega_n}
       \left(\frac{L\sin\xi}{D}\right)^{n-1}
 \le 8\sqrt n\left(\frac{L\sin\xi}{D}\right)^{n-1}.
\label{eq:cylinder}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cylinder"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cylinder</summary>

*Proof.* Condition on $Y=y$ with $0<|y-p|\le L$, and write $u=(y-p)/|y-p|$. If the event in the statement occurs, the component of $Z-q$ perpendicular to $u$ has length at most $L\sin\xi$. Both orientations allowed by the projective angle therefore lie in the same cylinder around the affine line $q+\mathbb Ru$. The projection of $B_D$ onto the direction $u$ has length $2D$. Every perpendicular slice of the cylinder has $(n-1)$-dimensional volume at most $\omega_{n-1}(L\sin\xi)^{n-1}$. Thus the allowed points $Z$ have volume at most $2D\omega_{n-1}(L\sin\xi)^{n-1}$. Divide by $|G|$ and then average over $Y$.

To obtain the last inequality, use the slicing identity $$\frac{\omega_n}{\omega_{n-1}}
 =\int_{-1}^1(1-t^2)^{(n-1)/2}\,dt
 \ge\frac{2\exp(-1/2)}{\sqrt n}.$$ Indeed, restrict the integral to $|t|\le n^{-1/2}$ and use $\log(1-1/n)\ge-1/(n-1)$. In particular, $\omega_{n-1}/\omega_n\le\sqrt n$. ◻

</details>

For a finite center list $P=(p_1,\ldots,p_N)$ and a point $y$ different from its entries, write $$\mathcal R_P(y)=(y-p_i:\ |y-p_i|\le b+3\eta)$$ for its labeled list of relevant residuals. For $\xi>0$, call a pair $(y,z)$ *$\xi$-bad* if some residual in $\mathcal R_P(y)$ and some residual in $\mathcal R_P(z)$ have projective angle at most $\xi$. This definition is symmetric in $y,z$ and retains repeated center labels. Recall $\gamma$ from <a href="#eq:rates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rates">[eq:rates]</a>, and put <a id="eq:target-count"></a>$$M_n=\left\lfloor\exp\left(\frac{n\log n}{16}\right)\right\rfloor.
\label{eq:target-count}$$

<a id="stmt-lem-targets"></a>
<div id="lem:targets" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Separated targets). *Fix $H>0$ and $k_0>0$. For all sufficiently large $n$, let $P=(p_1,\ldots,p_N)$ be a deterministic list satisfying $$N\le n^{k_0}\exp(\lambda_0n),$$ and let $G\subset B_D\setminus\{p_1,\ldots,p_N\}$ be measurable with $|G|\ge\omega_nD^n/4$. There are $M_n$ distinct points $y_1,\ldots,y_{M_n}\in G$ such that:*

1.  *no pair with distinct indices is $H\sqrt{\log n/n}$-bad;*

<a id="eq:bad-pair-rate"></a>
2.  *at most a fraction $10f_n$ of the unordered pairs with distinct indices is $4\sqrt\varepsilon$-bad, where $$f_n=8\sqrt n\,n^{2k_0}\exp(2\lambda_0n)
           \left(\frac{8\sqrt\varepsilon}{D}\right)^{n-1}
         =\exp\bigl(-\gamma n+O(\log n)\bigr).
    \label{eq:bad-pair-rate}$$*

*The constants and the dimension threshold are uniform in $P$ and $G$. The selected points can be fixed using only $P$ and $G$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-targets"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-targets</summary>

<a id="eq:target-pair"></a>
*Proof.* Sample $M_n$ points independently and uniformly from $G$. For independent sampled points $Y,Z$, take the union in Lemma <a href="#lem:cylinder" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cylinder">11</a> over all $N^2$ ordered pairs of center labels. Since $b+3\eta\le2$ for sufficiently large $n$, this gives $$\mathbb P\bigl((Y,Z)\text{ is $\xi$-bad}\bigr)
 \le8\sqrt n\,N^2
       \left(\frac{2\sin\xi}{D}\right)^{n-1}.
\label{eq:target-pair}$$ All relevant labels are included even though the relevant lists vary with $Y$ and $Z$. Repeated centers only repeat events in this union.

Set $t_n=H\sqrt{\log n/n}$. Multiply the right side of <a href="#eq:target-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-pair">[eq:target-pair]</a>, with $\sin t_n\le t_n$, by $\binom{M_n}{2}$. The logarithm of the resulting bound is at most $$-\frac38n\log n+\frac12n\log\log n+O_{H,k_0}(n),$$ which tends to $-\infty$. Thus the probability that any pair of sampled points is $t_n$-bad tends to zero, uniformly in the pattern. For $\xi=4\sqrt\varepsilon$, the inequality $\sin\xi\le4\sqrt\varepsilon$ instead bounds <a href="#eq:target-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-pair">[eq:target-pair]</a> by $f_n$. The expected fraction of these bad unordered pairs is at most $f_n$. Markov’s inequality shows that this fraction exceeds $10f_n$ with probability at most $1/10$. Consequently both conclusions hold with probability at least $9/10-o(1)>0$. The sampled points are distinct almost surely because uniform measure on $G$ has no atoms. Fix a successful list. No independence between the two separation properties is needed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### From targets to a small covering probability

We apply the preceding selection before exposing the Poisson process. For each selected target, Corollary <a href="#cor:witnesses" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:witnesses">10</a> supplies sets of slab normals whose simultaneous occupancy certifies that the target is uncovered. Binning their activities will let us combine many certificates without losing their angular separation.

<a id="stmt-prop-pattern-tail"></a>
<div id="prop:pattern-tail" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:pattern-tail"></a>
**Proposition 13** (Failure probability for one pattern). *There are constants $c_0,C_{\mathrm{tail}}>0$ and $n_0$ such that, whenever $n\ge n_0$ and $1\le R\le c_0n\log n$, every retained pattern $P\in\mathcal F_n$ of Proposition <a href="#prop:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:localization">3</a> satisfies $$\mathbb P\left(B_D\subseteq\bigcup_{p\in P}(p+K^+)\right)
 \le \exp\left[-\exp\bigl(\gamma n-C_{\mathrm{tail}}\log n\bigr)\right].
\label{eq:pattern-tail}$$ The constants are independent of the pattern, its repetitions, and $R$.*

</div>

The covering event is measurable. Indeed, the finite union of translates of $K^+$ is closed, so it covers $B_D$ exactly when it contains a fixed countable dense subset of $B_D$. At each such point, membership is a finite union of ball conditions and Poisson cap-avoidance events.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-pattern-tail"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-pattern-tail</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $P\in\mathcal F_n$ and its deterministic good set $G_P$. At each $y\in G_P$, Proposition <a href="#prop:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:localization">3</a> gives <a id="eq:target-residual-budget"></a>$$r_--2\eta\le |x_i|\le b+3\eta,\qquad
 m_y\le2C_{\mathrm{loc}}R,\qquad
 \sum_{i=1}^{m_y}w_a(|x_i|)\le2C_{\mathrm{loc}}R
\label{eq:target-residual-budget}$$ for its relevant residuals $x_i=y-p_i$. If this list is empty at any point of $G_P$, that point lies in none of the balls $p+(b+3\eta)B$, so the probability in <a href="#eq:pattern-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pattern-tail">[eq:pattern-tail]</a> is zero. We may assume all these lists are nonempty. For the range of $R$ in the proposition, $m_y\le n^2$ once $n$ is sufficiently large.

For each relevant residual, define the cutting cap $$C_i(y)=\{u\in S^{n-1}:|u\cdot x_i|>1+3\eta\}.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:caps">4</a>, applied with radial loss $10\log n/n$ and a fixed perturbation bound, gives $$s_i=-\log\min\{\mu(C_i(y)),1/2\}
       \le C_a w_a(|x_i|).$$ Consequently <a id="eq:target-cap-budget"></a>$$m_y+\sum_{i=1}^{m_y}s_i\le A_0R,
 \qquad A_0=2C_{\mathrm{loc}}(1+C_a).
\label{eq:target-cap-budget}$$ All assumptions of Corollary <a href="#cor:witnesses" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:witnesses">10</a> therefore hold uniformly in $y$ and $P$. Fix its constants $A,C_*$ and $H$ for this value of $A_0$.

<a id="eq:target-activities"></a>
The list-length estimate in Proposition <a href="#prop:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:localization">3</a> gives $N\le n^{k_0}\exp(\lambda_0n)$ for a fixed $k_0$, uniformly in the present range of $R$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:targets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:targets">12</a> to $G_P$ with the just-fixed $H$. At each of the resulting $M=M_n$ targets, choose the block partition and measurable witness sets from Corollary <a href="#cor:witnesses" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:witnesses">10</a>. Denote its injection count by $Z_y$, its activity by $\nu_y$, and the diagram sums by $D_{yz}$. The corollary gives $$\exp(-AR)\le\nu_y\le1,
 \qquad D_{yy}\le\nu_y^2\exp(C_*R).
\label{eq:target-activities}$$ Every pair of distinct selected targets satisfies $D_{yz}\le n^{-3}\nu_y\nu_z$. Moreover, $D_{yz}=0$ unless the pair is $4\sqrt\varepsilon$-bad. Our use of “at most” in the definition of badness makes both angular interfaces strict on its complement.

All choices so far depend only on $P$ and its targets. In particular, the witness sets are chosen before the Poisson process; their activities are deterministic intensities. There is no need to choose witness sets measurably at every point of $G_P$, since only the finite selected list is used. If $Z_y>0$, every relevant residual is cut by a slab of the process, and the other centers are too far from $y$ to contain it in a translate of $K^+$. Thus $Z_y>0$ certifies a hole.

<a id="eq:bin-bad-pairs"></a>
Divide the possible values of $-\log\nu_y$ in $[0,AR]$ into intervals of length $\log2$. At most $$Q=1+\left\lceil\frac{AR}{\log2}\right\rceil$$ bins suffice. A largest bin has $m\ge M/Q$ targets and, for some $\nu>0$, all its activities belong to $[\nu,2\nu]$. There are at most $10f_n\binom M2$ bad unordered pairs in the full list. Hence the chosen bin contains at most $$10f_nM(M-1)\le10f_nQ^2m^2
\label{eq:bin-bad-pairs}$$ bad ordered pairs with distinct indices. This bound allows the bin to contain every bad pair; no independence between bin membership and angles is asserted.

<a id="eq:bin-tail"></a>
Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:poisson" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:poisson">8</a> to the witnesses in this bin. Its numerator is at least $m^2\nu^2$. By <a href="#eq:target-activities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-activities">[eq:target-activities]</a> and <a href="#eq:bin-bad-pairs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bin-bad-pairs">[eq:bin-bad-pairs]</a>, its denominator before the factor $2$ is at most $$\sum_{y,z}D_{yz}
 \le4m\nu^2\exp(C_*R)
       +40m^2\nu^2n^{-3}Q^2f_n.$$ The first term counts diagonal pairs. Among distinct pairs, only the coarse bad pairs contribute. Coverage forces $Z_y=0$ at every target of the bin, so $$\mathbb P\left(B_D\subseteq\bigcup_{p\in P}(p+K^+)\right)
 \le\exp\left[-\frac1{
 8\bigl(\exp(C_*R)/m+10n^{-3}Q^2f_n\bigr)}\right].
\label{eq:bin-tail}$$

Now choose $$c_0=\frac1{64\max\{C_*,1\}}.$$ Uniformly for $1\le R\le c_0n\log n$, we have $\log Q=O(\log n)$ and, for sufficiently large $n$, $$m\exp(-C_*R)
 \ge\frac{M_n}{Q}\exp(-C_*R)
 \ge\exp\left(\frac{n\log n}{32}\right).$$ The first term inside the parentheses in <a href="#eq:bin-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bin-tail">[eq:bin-tail]</a> is therefore at most $\exp(-n\log n/32)$. By <a href="#eq:bad-pair-rate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bad-pair-rate">[eq:bad-pair-rate]</a>, the second is $\exp(-\gamma n+O(\log n))$. Since $\gamma$ is fixed, their sum is at most $\exp(-\gamma n+O(\log n))$; absorbing the fixed factor $8$ gives <a href="#eq:pattern-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pattern-tail">[eq:pattern-tail]</a>.

The order of the choices is fixed throughout: the geometric parameters come first, then the cap and block constants and $H$, then $c_0$, and finally the dimension threshold. Every estimate is uniform over the retained finite class. In particular, the auxiliary target selection introduces no dependence on the realization of $\Pi$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:assembly"></a>
### Completion of the proof

We now combine the estimates and specify their order of use. The numerical parameters <a href="#eq:fixed-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-constants">[eq:fixed-constants]</a> are already fixed. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:caps">4</a> with the shell and perturbation constants required by Proposition <a href="#prop:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:localization">3</a>. Its affinity and common-intersection constants determine the constants in Proposition <a href="#prop:blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:blocks">6</a>, including its radius cutoff. The radial cost estimate gives $A_0=2C_{\mathrm{loc}}(1+C_a)$ in the residual budget. For this value of $A_0$, Corollary <a href="#cor:witnesses" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:witnesses">10</a> fixes the cross-angle constant $H$ and the self-diagram coefficient $C_*$. Choose a positive rational number <a id="eq:choose-c"></a>$$\label{eq:choose-c}
 c\le\frac1{64\max(C_*,1)}.$$ Every constant in this order is independent of dimension. Increase $n_0$ so that all preceding estimates hold when $n\ge n_0$ and $R=c n\log n$. In particular, $R\ge1$, $R\le n^2$, and every relevant cap list has at most $n^2$ entries.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-pattern-tail-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-pattern-tail</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Use the deterministic class $\mathcal F_n$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:localization">3</a>, with $R=c n\log n$. That proposition and Proposition <a href="#prop:pattern-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pattern-tail">13</a> give $$\begin{aligned}
 &\mathbb P\left\{\text{some }P\in\mathcal F_n
             \text{ covers }B_D\text{ by translates of }K^+\right\}\\
 &\hspace{1cm}\le
 \exp\left(\exp(\lambda_0 n+O(\log n))
                -\exp(\gamma n-O(\log n))\right)=o(1).
\end{aligned}$$ The error bounds are uniform over this finite class. Since $\gamma>\lambda_0$, the negative term dominates. Lemma <a href="#lem:volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:volume">2</a> therefore guarantees a realization for which $|K|>2v\omega_n a^n$ and no retained pattern covers $B_D$ by translates of $K^+$.

Suppose that $K+\Lambda+T=\mathbb R^n$ with $|T|/\det\Lambda\le\rho_0$. Proposition <a href="#prop:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:localization">3</a> would give a retained pattern covering $B_D$ by $K^+$, a contradiction. Every covering thus has center intensity greater than $\rho_0$. Taking the infimum of its density yields $$\theta_T(K)=\theta_{\mathrm{per}}(K)\ge\rho_0|K|>R=c n\log n,$$ where Proposition <a href="#prop:periodization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:periodization">17</a> supplies the equality. The realization is a centrally symmetric convex body, as observed after <a href="#eq:buffered-body" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:buffered-body">[eq:buffered-body]</a>. Choose one for each $n\ge n_0$. ◻

</details>

The exclusion is simultaneous over all full-rank lattices and all finite coset lists. The lattice and the centers may depend on the chosen body: localization still places their rounded list in the class fixed before sampling. The periodization equivalence transfers this exclusion to unrestricted locally finite coverings without changing the body or the constant.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The optimal order for translative and lattice coverings

For a convex body $K$, define its single-lattice covering density by $$\theta_L(K)=\inf_{\Lambda:\ K+\Lambda=\mathbb R^n}
                   \frac{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(K)}{\det\Lambda}.$$ Every lattice covering is periodic, so $\theta_T(K)=\theta_{\mathrm{per}}(K)\le\theta_L(K)$. Let $\mathcal K^n$ denote the convex bodies in $\mathbb R^n$ and $\mathcal K^n_{\mathrm{sym}}$ their centrally symmetric members.

<a id="stmt-cor-sharp-order"></a>
<div id="cor:sharp-order" class="corollary">

**Corollary 14**. *As $n\to\infty$, each of the four quantities $$\begin{gathered}
 \sup_{K\in\mathcal K^n}\theta_T(K),\qquad
 \sup_{K\in\mathcal K^n_{\mathrm{sym}}}\theta_T(K),\\
 \sup_{K\in\mathcal K^n}\theta_L(K),\qquad
 \sup_{K\in\mathcal K^n_{\mathrm{sym}}}\theta_L(K)
\end{gathered}$$ has order $n\log n$, with absolute comparison constants.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-sharp-order"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-sharp-order</summary>

*Proof.* Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> gives the lower bound already among centrally symmetric bodies, and $\theta_T\le\theta_L$ transfers it to lattice coverings. Rogers’ bound <a href="#eq:rogers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rogers">[eq:rogers]</a> supplies the translative upper bound. The companion single-lattice theorem (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[OpenAI2026SingleLattice](#ref-OpenAI2026SingleLattice)] gives $\theta_L(K)\le Cn\log n$ for every convex body and every $n\ge2$, which supplies the two lattice upper bounds. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:encoding"></a>
### Rational polytopes and normal lists

The strict volume margin in the proof gives a polyhedral consequence of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> without changing its constant.

<a id="stmt-cor-rational-polytope"></a>
<div id="cor:rational-polytope" class="corollary">

**Corollary 15**. *With the same $c$ and $n_0$ as in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, for every $n\ge n_0$ there is a centrally symmetric polytope $P_n\subset\mathbb R^n$ with rational vertices such that $$\theta_T(P_n)>c n\log n.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-rational-polytope"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-rational-polytope</summary>

*Proof.* Fix the successful body $K$ in the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, and put $R=c n\log n$. It satisfies $|K|>2v\omega_na^n$, and no periodic covering by $K$ has center intensity at most $\rho_0=R/(2v\omega_na^n)$.

Enumerate the rational points of $\operatorname{int}K$ as $q_1,q_2,\ldots$ and set $$P_j=\operatorname{conv}\{\pm q_1,\ldots,\pm q_j\}.$$ These are centrally symmetric rational polytopes contained in $\operatorname{int}K$, and they increase with $j$. Their union is $\operatorname{int}K$: every interior point lies inside a sufficiently small simplex with rational vertices in $\operatorname{int}K$, and all its vertices eventually occur in the enumeration. Continuity of volume from below, together with the fact that the boundary of a convex body has measure zero, gives $|P_j|\to|K|$. Choose $j$ so that $|P_j|>2v\omega_na^n$; in particular, this $P_j$ has nonempty interior.

Every periodic covering by $P_j$ is a covering by $K$, because $P_j\subset K$. Its center intensity therefore exceeds $\rho_0$. Taking the infimum of the covering densities and using Proposition <a href="#prop:periodization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:periodization">17</a> yields $$\theta_T(P_j)=\theta_{\mathrm{per}}(P_j)
       \ge\rho_0|P_j|>R.$$ This argument uses both inclusion and the retained volume margin. ◻

</details>

A different finite description retains the spherical truncation $bB$ and makes all slab normals rational unit vectors. These bodies need not be polytopes.

<a id="stmt-prop-rational"></a>
<div id="prop:rational" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16**. *The bodies in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> can be chosen in the form $$K=bB\cap\bigcap_{j=1}^m\{x:|u_j\cdot x|\le1\},
 \qquad u_j\in\mathbb Q^n\cap S^{n-1},$$ with $m<\infty$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-rational"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-rational</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a successful finite normal list $U=(u_1,\ldots,u_m)$ from the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. The class $\mathcal F_n$ is finite and independent of $U$. For each $P\in\mathcal F_n$, select a point $y_P\in B_D$ outside all of its translates of $K^+(U)$. For every center $p$ in that list, either $|y_P-p|>b+3\eta$, or one of the normals satisfies $$|u_j\cdot(y_P-p)|>1+3\eta.$$ Each required inequality is strict. There are finitely many patterns, centers, and chosen certificates, so all these inequalities persist under a sufficiently small simultaneous perturbation of the normals. Empty patterns need no certificates.

The strict volume test persists as well. If $U'$ is a list of unit normals with $|u_j-u'_j|\le\delta$ for every $j$, then <a id="eq:volume-continuity"></a>$$\label{eq:volume-continuity}
 (1+b\delta)^{-1}K(U)\subset K(U')\subset(1+b\delta)K(U).$$ Indeed, if $x\in K(U)$, then $|x|\le b$ and $|u'_j\cdot x|\le1+b\delta$; scaling by $(1+b\delta)^{-1}$ proves the first inclusion. Interchanging $U$ and $U'$ proves the second. Volumes therefore converge as $\delta\to0$.

Rational unit vectors are dense in $S^{n-1}$ for $n\ge2$, by the stereographic parametrization $$t\longmapsto\frac{(2t,1-|t|^2)}{1+|t|^2},
 \qquad t\in\mathbb Q^{n-1},$$ and approximation of its omitted pole. Approximate all normals closely enough to preserve the holes and the strict volume test. The localization argument applies to every finite normal list, so the resulting rational list yields the same lower bound. ◻

</details>

One can specify a choice exactly by ordering the finite rational normal lists. Write each coordinate in reduced form with positive denominator, and let its height be the maximum of the absolute numerator and denominator. Order lists by their length plus maximum coordinate height (height $1$ for the empty list), breaking each finite tie lexicographically. The lists satisfying the strict volume test and every finite-pattern noncoverage test form a nonempty set, so they have a first member. This specifies a finite mathematical object; it does not assert an algorithm for deciding the volume comparisons or an effective bound for the first admissible dimension.

<a id="sec:periodization"></a>
### Covering density and periodization

The proof constructs bodies that exclude every finite union of lattice cosets. We verify that this gives the same lower bound for unrestricted translative coverings, with a precise density convention. This standard periodization principle is recorded in Prosanov (Prosanov 2021, Theorem 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Prosanov2021PackingCovering](#ref-Prosanov2021PackingCovering)], with attribution to Rogers. We give the finite-window argument to make its boundary and limiting conventions explicit.

Fix a convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ and write $V=\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(K)$. Use the definitions of $\theta_T$ and $\theta_{\mathrm{per}}$ from Section 1. For a locally finite covering center set $X$, the centered cube lower density replaces the $\limsup$ in <a href="#eq:unrestricted-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unrestricted-density">[eq:unrestricted-density]</a> by $\liminf$. The centered ball versions use $B_R$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(B_R)$ in place of $Q_R$ and $(2R)^n$.

<a id="stmt-prop-periodization"></a>
<div id="prop:periodization" class="proposition">

**Proposition 17** (Periodization). *For every convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, $$\theta_T(K)=\theta_{\mathrm{per}}(K).$$ The same infimum is obtained by replacing the centered cube upper density by centered cube lower density or centered Euclidean-ball lower or upper density. These are equalities of infima over coverings; the corresponding statistics of one covering need not agree.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-periodization"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-periodization</summary>

<a id="eq:density-window"></a>
*Proof.* Choose $r>0$ with $K\subset Q_r$, and let $X$ be a locally finite covering center set. For $a\in\mathbb R^n$ and $L>0$, the finite set $$S=X\cap(a+Q_{L+r})$$ covers $a+Q_L$ by translates of $K$. Indeed, if $y\in a+Q_L$ and $y=k+x$ with $k\in K$ and $x\in X$, then $x=y-k\in a+Q_{L+r}$. This holds on the boundary as well. Repeating $S$ with periods $2L\mathbb Z^n$ covers all of $\mathbb R^n$ because the cubes $a+Q_L+2Lz$ cover it. Reducing $S$ modulo this lattice gives $$\theta_{\mathrm{per}}(K)
 \le \frac{V\#(X\cap(a+Q_{L+r}))}{(2L)^n}.
 \label{eq:density-window}$$ Writing $R=L+r$, we obtain, for every $a$ and every $R>r$, <a id="eq:density-cubes"></a>$$\frac{V\#(X\cap(a+Q_R))}{(2R)^n}
 \ge \theta_{\mathrm{per}}(K)(1-r/R)^n.
 \label{eq:density-cubes}$$ This proves the required lower bound for both centered cube conventions.

For the ball conventions, fix $L>0$ and put $\beta=\sqrt n(L+r)$. Integrate <a href="#eq:density-window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:density-window">[eq:density-window]</a> over $a\in z+B_{R-\beta}$, where $R>\beta$. Any counted center belongs to $z+B_R$, and each such center is counted on a set of $a$ of volume at most $(2(L+r))^n$. Tonelli’s theorem therefore gives $$\frac{\theta_{\mathrm{per}}(K)(2L)^n}{V}\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(B_{R-\beta})
 \le \int_{z+B_{R-\beta}}\#(X\cap(a+Q_{L+r}))\,da
 \le (2(L+r))^n\#(X\cap(z+B_R)).$$ Consequently, uniformly in $z$, $$\frac{V\#(X\cap(z+B_R))}{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(B_R)}
 \ge \theta_{\mathrm{per}}(K)
       \left(\frac{L}{L+r}\right)^n(1-\beta/R)^n.$$ First let $R\to\infty$ with $L$ fixed, and then let $L\to\infty$. This proves the lower bound for the ball lower densities and hence also for the upper densities. No comparison of a ball with just one containing or contained cube is needed.

For the reverse inequality, consider a periodic center set $X=\Lambda+T$ with distinct cosets. Let $F$ be a bounded half-open fundamental parallelepiped of $\Lambda$ and choose $d>0$ with $F\subset Q_d\cap B_d$. For each fixed $t\in T$, the cells $t+\lambda+F$ partition space up to null boundaries. For $R>d$ and $W_R=Q_R$ or $B_R$, the cells based at points of $t+\Lambda$ in $z+W_R$ contain $z+W_{R-d}$ up to a null set and are contained in $z+W_{R+d}$. Thus $$\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(W_{R-d})\le
 \det\Lambda\,\#((t+\Lambda)\cap(z+W_R))
 \le\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(W_{R+d}).$$ Divide by $\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(W_R)$ and sum over the distinct cosets. Every stated center-count intensity is therefore $|T|/\det\Lambda$, uniformly in $z$; the corresponding covering density is $V|T|/\det\Lambda$. The finite constant $d$ may depend on the lattice; no bound uniform over lattices is needed. Since periodic center sets are locally finite, restricting each unrestricted infimum to these competitors proves the reverse inequality. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
###### Average overlap multiplicity.

One customary definition uses the lower ball density of the overlap multiplicity (Prosanov 2021, sec. 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Prosanov2021PackingCovering](#ref-Prosanov2021PackingCovering)]. It agrees with the convention above. For either choice of $W_R$, put $$A_W(X,R)=\sum_{x\in X}\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}((K+x)\cap W_R).$$ Choose $s>0$ such that $K$ is contained in the radius-$s$ ball of the norm defining $W_R$. Local finiteness gives, for $R>s$, $$V\#(X\cap W_{R-s})\le A_W(X,R)
 \le V\#(X\cap W_{R+s}).$$ Replacing $R$ by $R+s$ or $R-s$ does not change the liminf or limsup of $\#(X\cap W_R)/\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(W_R)$: substitute the shifted radius and use $(R\pm s)^n/R^n\to1$. The lower and upper limits of $A_W(X,R)/\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(W_R)$ therefore equal the corresponding center-count intensities multiplied by $V$. This counts each overlapping translate separately; the volume of the covered union is not the density.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
###### Transfer of the lower bound.

In particular, any inequality $\theta_{\mathrm{per}}(K)>R$ established by the finite-coset argument implies $\theta_T(K)>R$ for the same body and the same constant. This uses equality of the infima and makes no claim that an individual covering can be repeated at exactly its original density. For every $\epsilon>0$, a covering of finite density $D$ does imply existence of a periodic covering of density less than $D+\epsilon$.

Below the vertices: $d_P(4) = 2$, $d_P(5) = 2$.\n- Right (labeled \"optional diagonal match\"): a single isolated vertex 6 with a dashed directed loop at the top. Below the vertex: $d_P(6) = 0$."
